Step 8 of 12
ACTIVATION 7 - the Avatar's fourth toughness point, and its own death. Pay 1 life (you 39), sacrifice Insect H (counterless, toughness 1): the Avatar gains you 1 life (you 40) and each opponent loses 1 (opponents 33 each). Critically, the Avatar is still on the battlefield when that sacrifice happens - the cost is paid before the ability resolves - so this drain is collected in full. The ability then resolves, putting the FOURTH -1/-1 counter on South Wind Avatar, making it -1/0. State-based actions put it into the graveyard.
South Wind Avatar dies. Its own ability reads 'Whenever ANOTHER creature you control dies', so its own death does not match its trigger event and it does not trigger for itself - no final life, no final drain. This is a permanent fact about the Avatar and it is load-bearing for the ceiling proved at step 11: the Avatar can never be its own drain. The Nest of Scarabs trigger from that fourth counter still resolves and makes Insect J - a body that is created AFTER the Avatar has already been put into the graveyard. Draw (library 53). The lifegain/drain engine is now off permanently: every later sacrifice gains nothing and drains nothing.
2 citations on the record
Step 9 of 12
ACTIVATION 8 and the terminus, narrated in the order the rules actually run. Activating an ability follows the 601.2 sequence (step 2's 115.1c points at 602.2b for exactly this), and in that sequence targets are ANNOUNCED at 601.2c and the total cost is paid five lettered steps later at 601.2h, with 601.2d (dividing or distributing an effect), 601.2e (the legality check), 601.2f (determining the total cost) and 601.2g (activating mana abilities) all intervening. The only thing the argument needs is the DIRECTION - announcement precedes payment - and that direction is what 601.2c and 601.2h jointly fix. So at the moment targets are chosen for activation 8, Insect J is still on the battlefield and IS a legal target; Yawgmoth is not (step 2). Choosing zero targets is therefore a CHOICE the ability permits ('up to one target creature'), not a compulsion forced by an empty target pool. It is the correct choice: had J been targeted, paying the cost would then have sacrificed J, moving it off the battlefield (step 1's 701.21a), and on resolution the ability's only target would have been illegal - so the ability would not have resolved at all and you would have lost the card. With no target chosen, the cost is paid: 1 life (you 39) and Insect J sacrificed. No Avatar is on the battlefield, so no lifegain trigger and no drain. The ability resolves; no counter is placed, so Nest of Scarabs does not trigger and no replacement Insect is created; you draw a card (library 53 -> 52).
LOOP ENDS. You now control only Yawgmoth, Nest of Scarabs and three Swamps - no other creature. The activation cost is 'Pay 1 life, Sacrifice another creature'; with no other creature on the battlefield, that cost is unpayable and cannot be paid, so there is no ninth activation. Note what the terminus rests on: the SACRIFICE COST becoming unpayable, not the target pool being empty. The engine is bounded by BODIES - not by life, which was net zero for as long as the Avatar lived and then cost 1 per activation out of a 40 life total.

DISPOSE OF PROLIFERATE - Yawgmoth's OTHER activated ability, which the scenario's mana line invites. Yawgmoth, Thran Physician also reads '{B}{B}, Discard a card: Proliferate.' What that can reach here: Grizzly Bears while it carries one counter, and South Wind Avatar once it carries counters. It can NEVER reach one of your Insects - a 1/1 Insect that takes a -1/-1 counter is 0/0 and is put into the graveyard by a state-based action before any player receives priority to activate anything (step 6's 704.5f), so no Insect ever bears a counter at a time when proliferate could be activated. No player here has poison or any other counter.
It does NOT change either number. A proliferated -1/-1 counter is a counter YOU put on a creature, so Nest of Scarabs triggers and makes a token for free - no sacrifice, no life paid (step 5). But the token comes out of the SAME fixed pool the ceiling is computed from: the only creatures that can absorb a counter without costing you a body are Grizzly Bears and the Avatar, and their printed toughness caps that pool at 2 + 4 = 6 counters no matter who places them. A counter proliferate places is a counter no later activation can place, so it buys a body 1-for-1 out of capacity already counted at step 11. Activations stay at 8. Drains stay at 7 as well, because proliferate produces no death of a creature YOU control that gains nonzero life: it cannot kill an Insect (it can never target one), Grizzly Bears is an opponent's, and if it is used to finish the Avatar, the Avatar's death never triggers the Avatar's own ability (step 8). So: {B}{B}{B} on the board is real and the ability is genuinely activatable, and the answer to (a) and (b) is unchanged at 8 activations and 7 drains. (Sister stack 002, where proliferate DOES move the drain count from 7 to 8, differs precisely because that board's drain fires on your creatures dying at any toughness; here a counter-killed body gains 0 and drains nothing.)

8 completed Yawgmoth activations, ended by body exhaustion (no creature other than Yawgmoth left to sacrifice - not life, not library). South Wind Avatar's dies-trigger fired 8 times but produced only 7 nonzero lifegain events: a sacrificed counterless Insect dies at last known toughness 1 (gain 1), while an Insect killed by its own -1/-1 counter dies at toughness 0 (gain 0, and per CR 119.9 that is not a lifegain event, so no drain). Spellbook's 'Near-infinite' tag is wrong for this board.

Persist + Nest of Scarabs

does a creature that ENTERS with a -1/-1 counter trigger the Nest?

CR 122.6 is broader than it first reads: a reference to putting counters on an object covers an object given counters as it enters, not only counters placed on something already sitting there. Persist names no player, so 122.6a makes you the one placing it — which is precisely what Nest of Scarabs asks for. The holding: the Nest triggers and you get an Insect, even though the returned Fume Spitter is back in the graveyard before that trigger reaches the stack. It had already triggered.

The board and the walk-through — 6 step(s)
The board
YouPermanents Nest of ScarabsLands Swamp, Swamp, SwampGraveyard Fume Spitter (creature card, nonlegendary)Mana {B}{B}{B} from three untapped Swamps (Persist's cost is already paid)
Opponent A — 40 lifeno permanents
Opponent B — 40 lifeno permanents
Opponent C — 40 lifeno permanents
Opponent D — 40 lifeno permanents
On the stack, top first
  1. Persist — 'Return target nonlegendary creature card from your graveyard to the battlefield with a -1/-1 counter on it.' Cost already paid ({1}{B}). Target chosen: Fume Spitter in your graveyard.
    controlled by you
    — resolves first
1
Step 1

Persist resolves. Its one target is rechecked for legality first: Fume Spitter is still a nonlegendary creature card in your graveyard, so the target is legal and the spell resolves. You follow its instruction in the order written: return Fume Spitter from your graveyard to the battlefield with a -1/-1 counter on it. Persist does not name a controller, so Fume Spitter enters under YOUR control (you are the player the effect instructed to put it onto the battlefield). Persist itself is then put into your graveyard as the final part of its resolution.

2
Step 2

THE RULING, and it is decided by one rule: 122.6. Nest of Scarabs asks 'Whenever you put one or more -1/-1 counters on a creature.' Rule 122.6 says that a spell or ability referring to counters being put on an object refers BOTH to putting counters on it while it is on the battlefield AND to an object that is given counters as it enters the battlefield. So 'entering with a -1/-1 counter' is not a separate, exempt category — it is inside the reference. The second half of the question, WHO put it, is answered by 122.6a: the effect may specify a player, and Persist does not, so the object's controller puts those counters on it. Fume Spitter's controller is you (step 1). Therefore YOU put one -1/-1 counter on a creature, and Nest of Scarabs' trigger condition is satisfied. ANSWER: YES, Nest of Scarabs triggers.

3
Step 3

The trigger fires. The game event (you putting one -1/-1 counter on a creature) matches Nest of Scarabs' trigger condition, so the ability triggers automatically at that moment. It does nothing yet and is not yet on the stack.

4
Step 4

State-based actions are checked BEFORE the waiting trigger goes on the stack. Fume Spitter is a 1/1 with one -1/-1 counter on it, which subtracts from power and toughness, making it 0/0. A creature with toughness 0 or less is put into its owner's graveyard — Fume Spitter dies immediately, without ever having been available to sacrifice. The check is then repeated; nothing more is performed, and the Nest of Scarabs trigger is put on the stack. Fume Spitter dying does NOT undo the trigger: it had already triggered in step 3, and the trigger is an independent object on the stack that no longer depends on its source event or on Fume Spitter.

5
Step 5

You receive priority and pass; every opponent passes in succession (the scenario's stated assumption), so the Nest of Scarabs trigger resolves. It creates that many — one — 1/1 black Insect creature tokens: put one token with those characteristics onto the battlefield under your control. The ability then leaves the stack.

6
Step 6

THE CONTRAST THE QUESTION ASKS FOR: would the answer differ if an effect instead placed a -1/-1 counter on a creature ALREADY on the battlefield? No. The answer is YES in both cases, and 122.6 is precisely the rule that makes them the same — it names both halves in one sentence. What changes is only which rule identifies YOU as the actor. For a creature already on the battlefield, an ability you control instructs you to put the counter and you follow that instruction (608.2c) — that is the route stacks 001-005 take. For a permanent entering with counters, no instruction is being followed against an on-battlefield object, so the actor comes from 122.6a's default: the object's controller. THE ONE PLACE THE TWO GENUINELY DIVERGE, and a pilot should know it: because 122.6a hands the counters to the object's CONTROLLER, a nonlegendary creature returned to the battlefield under an OPPONENT's control with a -1/-1 counter would have that counter put on by that opponent, not by you, and Nest of Scarabs — which says 'whenever YOU put' — would not trigger. In this scenario Fume Spitter enters under your control, so that caveat does not bite.

Resolve
Step 1 of 6
Persist resolves. Its one target is rechecked for legality first: Fume Spitter is still a nonlegendary creature card in your graveyard, so the target is legal and the spell resolves. You follow its instruction in the order written: return Fume Spitter from your graveyard to the battlefield with a -1/-1 counter on it. Persist does not name a controller, so Fume Spitter enters under YOUR control (you are the player the effect instructed to put it onto the battlefield). Persist itself is then put into your graveyard as the final part of its resolution.
Fume Spitter is on the battlefield under your control with one -1/-1 counter on it. Persist is in your graveyard. Nothing has been targeted or damaged; life totals are unchanged at 40 apiece.
4 citations on the record
Step 2 of 6
THE RULING, and it is decided by one rule: 122.6. Nest of Scarabs asks 'Whenever you put one or more -1/-1 counters on a creature.' Rule 122.6 says that a spell or ability referring to counters being put on an object refers BOTH to putting counters on it while it is on the battlefield AND to an object that is given counters as it enters the battlefield. So 'entering with a -1/-1 counter' is not a separate, exempt category — it is inside the reference. The second half of the question, WHO put it, is answered by 122.6a: the effect may specify a player, and Persist does not, so the object's controller puts those counters on it. Fume Spitter's controller is you (step 1). Therefore YOU put one -1/-1 counter on a creature, and Nest of Scarabs' trigger condition is satisfied. ANSWER: YES, Nest of Scarabs triggers.
The trigger condition of Nest of Scarabs is met. Note the citation this step deliberately does NOT lean on: sibling stacks 001-005 reach 'you are the actor' through 608.2c, because there the counter is placed by an ability you control following its instructions on a creature already on the battlefield. That reasoning does not by itself reach a permanent entering with counters — no instruction is being followed against an object on the battlefield at that moment. It is 122.6 that brings the entering case inside the phrase, and 122.6a that names you rather than 608.2c. Both roads arrive at the same place here, but only 122.6/122.6a is load-bearing for THIS scenario.
4 citations on the record
Step 3 of 6
The trigger fires. The game event (you putting one -1/-1 counter on a creature) matches Nest of Scarabs' trigger condition, so the ability triggers automatically at that moment. It does nothing yet and is not yet on the stack.
One Nest of Scarabs trigger is waiting to be put on the stack. 'That many' is locked to the number of -1/-1 counters in the event: ONE.
1 citation on the record
Step 4 of 6
State-based actions are checked BEFORE the waiting trigger goes on the stack. Fume Spitter is a 1/1 with one -1/-1 counter on it, which subtracts from power and toughness, making it 0/0. A creature with toughness 0 or less is put into its owner's graveyard — Fume Spitter dies immediately, without ever having been available to sacrifice. The check is then repeated; nothing more is performed, and the Nest of Scarabs trigger is put on the stack. Fume Spitter dying does NOT undo the trigger: it had already triggered in step 3, and the trigger is an independent object on the stack that no longer depends on its source event or on Fume Spitter.
Fume Spitter is in your graveyard. Your battlefield: Nest of Scarabs, three untapped Swamps. Stack: one Nest of Scarabs trigger. This is the pilot's real lesson — Persist on a 1-toughness creature returns it and kills it in the same breath, and what you actually keep is the Insect.
5 citations on the record
Step 5 of 6
You receive priority and pass; every opponent passes in succession (the scenario's stated assumption), so the Nest of Scarabs trigger resolves. It creates that many — one — 1/1 black Insect creature tokens: put one token with those characteristics onto the battlefield under your control. The ability then leaves the stack.
One 1/1 black Insect creature token enters the battlefield under your control. State-based actions are checked again and none apply (the Insect is 1/1, nothing else is on the battlefield to check). You receive priority in your precombat main phase with the stack empty.
4 citations on the record
Step 6 of 6
THE CONTRAST THE QUESTION ASKS FOR: would the answer differ if an effect instead placed a -1/-1 counter on a creature ALREADY on the battlefield? No. The answer is YES in both cases, and 122.6 is precisely the rule that makes them the same — it names both halves in one sentence. What changes is only which rule identifies YOU as the actor. For a creature already on the battlefield, an ability you control instructs you to put the counter and you follow that instruction (608.2c) — that is the route stacks 001-005 take. For a permanent entering with counters, no instruction is being followed against an on-battlefield object, so the actor comes from 122.6a's default: the object's controller. THE ONE PLACE THE TWO GENUINELY DIVERGE, and a pilot should know it: because 122.6a hands the counters to the object's CONTROLLER, a nonlegendary creature returned to the battlefield under an OPPONENT's control with a -1/-1 counter would have that counter put on by that opponent, not by you, and Nest of Scarabs — which says 'whenever YOU put' — would not trigger. In this scenario Fume Spitter enters under your control, so that caveat does not bite.
Ruling for the deck: Persist IS a legitimate ignition source for Nest of Scarabs — the -1/-1 counter it brings along counts as you putting a counter on a creature, and the Nest pays out one Insect. The goldfish_targets.json listing of Persist as an ignition source is NOT overstated on the rules. The honest caveat is a different one and it is about the BODY, not the trigger: Persist returning a 1-toughness creature (Fume Spitter) yields the Insect and immediately loses the creature to state-based actions, so it ignites without leaving a sacrifice body behind.
3 citations on the record

YES — Nest of Scarabs triggers, and rule 122.6 is what decides it: a reference to counters being put on an object covers an object that is GIVEN counters as it enters the battlefield, not only counters placed on something already there. Persist names no player, so 122.6a assigns the counter to Fume Spitter's controller, which is you — satisfying 'whenever you put one or more -1/-1 counters on a creature.' You get one 1/1 black Insect token. The counter also kills the creature it rode in on: Fume Spitter is a 1/1, enters as a 0/0, and is put into the graveyard by state-based actions before the trigger even reaches the stack — the trigger is unaffected because it had already triggered. The answer does NOT differ for a counter placed on a creature already on the battlefield: that case is YES too, via 608.2c naming you as the actor instead of 122.6a. The single divergence between the two cases is controller-dependent — a creature entering under an opponent's control with a -1/-1 counter has that counter put on by THAT player, so 'whenever you put' would not trigger; that does not apply here.

Mirkwood Bats + Yawgmoth + Nest of Scarabs

does one activation pay the drain twice?

Savour the order of operations. A cost is paid after the ability is already the topmost object on the stack (CR 602.2a into 601.2h), so the trigger the sacrifice causes lands above it and resolves first. One activation paid with a token therefore pays Mirkwood Bats twice: once for the sacrifice, once for the creation two resolutions later. One caveat for the record — a token killed by its own counter is put into the graveyard by the game rather than sacrificed by you (CR 704.5f), and pays the Bats on creation only.

The board and the walk-through — 11 step(s)
The board
YouCreatures Yawgmoth, Thran Physician, Mirkwood Bats, Insect token BPermanents Nest of ScarabsLands Swamp, Swamp, SwampAlready paid Insect token AMana {B}{B}{B} from three untapped Swamps
Opponent A — 40 lifeGrizzly Bears (2/2, untapped, no counters)
Opponent B — 40 lifeno permanents
Opponent C — 40 lifeno permanents
Opponent D — 40 lifeno permanents
On the stack, top first
  1. Yawgmoth, Thran Physician's activated ability — 'Pay 1 life, Sacrifice another creature: Put a -1/-1 counter on up to one target creature and draw a card.' Cost already paid: 1 life paid (you are at 39) and Insect token A sacrificed. Target chosen: Grizzly Bears.
    controlled by you
    — resolves first
1
Step 1

Fix the reading and restore the trigger the stack picture omits. THE READING: the scenario's board annotates 'Insect token A' as ALREADY SACRIFICED to pay the cost of the ability now on the stack, so the pilot enters this resolution holding exactly one live Insect (token B) plus Mirkwood Bats (2/3) and Yawgmoth (2/4). THE OMISSION: sacrificing Insect A was a sacrifice of a token while Mirkwood Bats was on the battlefield, so Bats has already triggered and that trigger is not shown. Establish precisely when it went on the stack, because the whole question turns on the two halves of Bats' condition arriving at different times. Activating Yawgmoth's ability put the ability itself on the stack FIRST (602.2a) and the activation cost -- 1 life and 'Sacrifice another creature' -- was paid LAST, as the final step of the activation process (602.2b routing to 601.2h). So Insect A was sacrificed (701.21a) while Yawgmoth's ability was already the topmost object on the stack. Nothing happens at the moment an ability triggers; the Bats trigger waits and is put on the stack the next time a player would receive priority (117.2a), which is immediately after the activation completes, and it becomes the topmost object (603.3) -- i.e. ABOVE Yawgmoth's ability.

2
Step 2

Bats trigger #1 resolves: 'each opponent loses 1 life.' Four opponents, four separate life losses. Bats grants YOU no life -- unlike the death-metering drainer of stack 003, which gained you 1 per drain.

3
Step 3

Yawgmoth's activated ability resolves. Its target is rechecked first: Grizzly Bears is still on the battlefield and unchanged, so it is still legal (608.2b). Put a -1/-1 counter on Grizzly Bears and draw a card. The counter subtracts from power and toughness (122.1a), so Bears is 1/1.

4
Step 4

ANSWER (a). State-based actions find nothing (Bears is 1/1), the Nest of Scarabs trigger is put on the stack, all players pass in succession per the scenario's stated assumption (117.4), and it resolves: exactly ONE -1/-1 counter was placed, so Nest creates 'that many' = one 1/1 black Insect token (701.7a). Creating it is the OTHER half of Bats' condition, so Bats triggers a second time; that trigger then resolves for another 1 life from each opponent. On the singular wording: Bats reads 'a token', and 603.2c settles both directions -- an ability triggers only once each time its trigger event occurs, but repeatedly if ONE event contains multiple occurrences. So a Nest trigger that placed N counters at once and created N tokens in a single event would trigger Bats N times, once per token; every Nest trigger in this line places exactly one counter and creates exactly one token, so each is worth exactly one Bats trigger. THEREFORE: one Yawgmoth activation paid with an Insect token and placing a counter produces TWO Bats triggers -- one for the sacrifice, one for the creation -- and they are NOT simultaneous and never sit on the stack together. The sacrifice trigger was on the stack ABOVE the activated ability and resolved before it (step 1); the creation trigger did not exist until Nest's own trigger resolved, two resolutions later. An activation paid with a token that places no counter is worth exactly ONE (the sacrifice half only).

5
Step 5

The precision that separates this drainer from stacks 002 and 003: a counter-KILLED Insect pays Bats NOTHING. When a -1/-1 counter drops a 1/1 Insect to 0/0 it is put into its owner's graveyard as a state-based action (704.5f). That is a death, and 002/003's drainers meter exactly that. It is not a sacrifice: to sacrifice is for the permanent's CONTROLLER to move it to the graveyard (701.21a), an action a player takes to pay a cost or follow an instruction, whereas 704.5f is the game putting it there automatically. Bats' condition is 'create or sacrifice', so a counter-kill matches neither half. Only the creation half applies to those tokens -- they paid Bats once, when they were made.

6
Step 6

Establish the engine's bound before running it, and answer the 2/3 question. Each activation spends one body (the sacrifice) and Nest returns at most one, so the loop is body-neutral only while a counter can land on a creature that is NOT one of your own Insects. The available counter sinks are: (i) Grizzly Bears, an opponent's creature -- two counter-placement events, the second killing it, and both are free to you because it is not your body; (ii) Mirkwood Bats itself. THIS IS WHERE THE 2/3 CHANGES THE BOUND. A 1/1 drainer (stack 003's) dies to its first counter and can never be a sink. Bats at 2/3 absorbs TWO -1/-1 counters and is 0/1 -- toughness 1, above 0, so 704.5f does not apply and it keeps draining (122.1a). Those two counters are two extra body-neutral counter events, each worth a free Nest token and therefore a free Bats trigger. A third counter would make Bats -1/0 and kill it, so two is the cap. (iii) Yawgmoth cannot be a sink at all: he is a Human Cleric and has protection from Humans, so his own ability -- an ability from a Human source -- can never target him (702.16b). And 'up to one target creature' permits choosing zero targets (115.6), so the pilot is never forced to put a counter anywhere.

7
Step 7

Activation #2, spending the first of Bats' own two counter slots. Pay 1 life, sacrifice Insect B (a token) -- Bats triggers, and by step 1's sequencing that trigger goes on the stack above the ability and resolves first. Target: Mirkwood Bats itself (a legal target -- it has no protection or hexproof, and you may target your own creature). Bats trigger resolves; then the ability resolves, putting a -1/-1 counter on Bats (2/3 -> 1/2, alive) and drawing a card. SBAs are performed and only then is the resulting Nest trigger put on the stack (117.5); nothing dies. Nest resolves, creating Insect D, and Bats triggers on the creation.

8
Step 8

Now spend the three Swamps the scenario deliberately supplies -- Yawgmoth's -1/-1 ability costs no mana, so the only mana sink is his second ability: '{B}{B}, Discard a card: Proliferate.' Tap two Swamps, discard a card drawn earlier. Proliferate lets you CHOOSE any number of permanents that have a counter and give each one more of a kind already there (701.34a), so you choose exactly Grizzly Bears and Mirkwood Bats and nothing else. Bears goes to two counters (0/0); Bats goes to two counters (0/1, alive). This is one event that puts a -1/-1 counter on TWO different creatures, so Nest of Scarabs -- 'whenever you put one or more -1/-1 counters on A CREATURE' -- triggers twice, once per creature, by 603.2c's multiple-occurrences clause. SBAs run before those triggers go on the stack: Bears is 0/0 and is put into its OWNER's graveyard (704.5f); Bats at 0/1 survives. The two Nest triggers then resolve one at a time, each creating one token, each paying Bats once.

9
Step 9

The terminal phase: every external sink is gone, so each remaining activation must aim its counter at one of your own Insects (or at nothing). Aiming it at an Insect is strictly better than aiming at nothing -- the counter-kill itself pays Bats nothing (step 5), but it triggers Nest, and the token Nest makes IS a creation, worth one Bats trigger. So each of these activations is still worth TWO Bats triggers (sacrifice + creation) while costing a net one body. Run it three times. Activation #3: pay 1 life, sacrifice Insect C (Bats trigger), target Insect D; D becomes 0/0, SBAs put it in the graveyard and it then ceases to exist, and only then does the Nest trigger go on the stack (117.5, 704.5f); Nest creates Insect G (Bats trigger). Activation #4: sacrifice Insect E, target Insect F, Nest creates Insect H -- identical shape. Activation #5: sacrifice Insect G, target Insect H, Nest creates Insect I -- identical shape.

10
Step 10

Activation #6, the last one, and the two lines the pilot must decline. Pay 1 life, sacrifice Insect I -- a token, so Bats triggers one final time. For the counter, the only remaining creatures are Yawgmoth (untargetable by his own ability, step 6) and Mirkwood Bats at 0/1. Targeting Bats would be a THIRD counter on it: Bats becomes -1/0 and is put into the graveyard by state-based action before the resulting Nest trigger is ever put on the stack (704.5f), so the token that trigger creates would be created with no Bats on the battlefield and would pay nothing -- the option is worth exactly zero and costs the drainer. The pilot therefore chooses zero targets, which 'up to one target creature' expressly permits (115.6): no counter, no Nest trigger, no token. The engine also cannot continue past this: the only creatures left are Yawgmoth, who cannot pay his own cost ('Sacrifice ANOTHER creature'), and Bats, which is not a token -- sacrificing Bats would pay Bats nothing and remove the drainer, so it is declined too.

11
Step 11

ANSWER (b) and the final state-based-action check. Ledger of Bats triggers, by half. SACRIFICE half -- six tokens were sacrificed to Yawgmoth's cost: Insect A (pre-resolution, step 1), B, C, E, G, I. CREATION half -- seven tokens were created by Nest of Scarabs: C and D from the two activations that used an external/Bats sink, E and F from the single proliferate (two Nest triggers), and G, H, I from the three terminal activations. Six plus seven is THIRTEEN Bats triggers, each draining every opponent for 1 (119.3). Note what did NOT pay: Grizzly Bears' death and the three Insects killed by counters (D, F, H) are four deaths worth nothing to Bats -- but only THREE of those four are what a 'you control' death-metering drainer would have banked, because Grizzly Bears is an opponent's creature. Zulaport Cutthroat (stack 003) reads 'Whenever this creature or another creature you control dies' and Bastion of Remembrance (stack 002) reads 'Whenever a creature you control dies'; the Bears is not a creature you control, so its death does not match either ability's trigger event and neither ability triggers on it (603.2); the word 'you' on each of those objects refers to that object's controller -- you -- and not to opponent A (109.5). They bank the three counter-killed Insects D, F and H, which are tokens you control. The one drainer that would see all four is stack 004's Blood Artist, which is controller-agnostic -- 'Whenever this creature or another creature dies' carries no controller restriction, so the Bears' death does pay it (though it drains one 'target player', not each opponent). Final SBA check: every opponent is at 27, well above 0, so no player loses the game (704.5a); you are at 34 and drew only 6 cards from a 60-card library. Every Insect token that reached a graveyard, whether sacrificed or counter-killed, then ceased to exist (704.5d), which is why nine token deaths leave no trace in any graveyard.

Only the FIRST of Bats' two counters is a counter an activation aimed at it; the second arrives from the proliferate, which can only grow a permanent that ALREADY carries a counter (step 8), and no Insect of yours can ever carry a -1/-1 counter and live -- so that second counter never had an Insect alternative to be spared from. Nor does this line spare Insects at all: it counter-kills THREE of your own tokens (D, F and H, step 9) where the 1/1 board's best line kills two, and three is forced in every optimal line here, not a choice. What the 2/3 body buys is ACTIVATIONS, not bodies -- the engine therefore runs SIX Yawgmoth activations instead of four, and every activation pays the sacrifice half exactly once, because sacrificing a token IS this ability's cost. THE +3 CREATIONS: two of them are the Nest tokens those two extra activations make (five token-creating activations here against three, since the last activation in either line targets nothing and makes nothing), and the third is the proliferate -- Bats carrying counters gives proliferate a SECOND permanent to grow, so one proliferate yields two Nest triggers on this board against one on the 1/1 board. None of the +5 comes from the counter-killed tokens: those pay the creation half only and are never sacrificed (steps 5 and 9), so no token this line kills with a counter is counted twice.

Proliferate lets you CHOOSE any number of permanents that have a counter and give each one more of a kind already there (701.34a), so you choose exactly Grizzly Bears and Mirkwood Bats and nothing else. Bears goes to two counters (0/0); Bats goes to two counters (0/1, alive). This is one event that puts a -1/-1 counter on TWO different creatures, so Nest of Scarabs -- 'whenever you put one or more -1/-1 counters on A CREATURE' -- triggers twice, once per creature, by 603.2c's multiple-occurrences clause. SBAs run before those triggers go on the stack: Bears is 0/0 and is put into its OWNER's graveyard (704.5f); Bats at 0/1 survives. The two Nest triggers then resolve one at a time, each creating one token, each paying Bats once.
Two tokens created (Insect E, Insect F) for two mana and a discard, with NO sacrifice spent -- two Bats triggers for free. Opponents 36 -> 34 each; you 38, hand 1, library 58. Grizzly Bears is dead and in opponent A's graveyard; its death pays Bats nothing (step 5). Bats 0/1 with two counters -- its sink capacity is now exhausted. Bodies: Insects C, D, E, F. One Swamp untapped, which can no longer buy anything (proliferate costs {B}{B}). Bats triggers so far: 6. Table life removed: 24.
3 citations on the record
Step 9 of 11
The terminal phase: every external sink is gone, so each remaining activation must aim its counter at one of your own Insects (or at nothing). Aiming it at an Insect is strictly better than aiming at nothing -- the counter-kill itself pays Bats nothing (step 5), but it triggers Nest, and the token Nest makes IS a creation, worth one Bats trigger. So each of these activations is still worth TWO Bats triggers (sacrifice + creation) while costing a net one body. Run it three times. Activation #3: pay 1 life, sacrifice Insect C (Bats trigger), target Insect D; D becomes 0/0, SBAs put it in the graveyard and it then ceases to exist, and only then does the Nest trigger go on the stack (117.5, 704.5f); Nest creates Insect G (Bats trigger). Activation #4: sacrifice Insect E, target Insect F, Nest creates Insect H -- identical shape. Activation #5: sacrifice Insect G, target Insect H, Nest creates Insect I -- identical shape.
Six more Bats triggers across three activations. Opponents 34 -> 28 each; you 38 -> 35 (three more life paid). Library 58 -> 55, hand 4. Bodies fall 4 -> 3 -> 2 -> 1: only Insect I remains. Bats triggers so far: 12. Table life removed: 48. Note the shape: each of these activations kills two of your tokens and makes one, so the engine is now net -1 body per activation and is visibly winding down.
2 citations on the record
Step 10 of 11
Activation #6, the last one, and the two lines the pilot must decline. Pay 1 life, sacrifice Insect I -- a token, so Bats triggers one final time. For the counter, the only remaining creatures are Yawgmoth (untargetable by his own ability, step 6) and Mirkwood Bats at 0/1. Targeting Bats would be a THIRD counter on it: Bats becomes -1/0 and is put into the graveyard by state-based action before the resulting Nest trigger is ever put on the stack (704.5f), so the token that trigger creates would be created with no Bats on the battlefield and would pay nothing -- the option is worth exactly zero and costs the drainer. The pilot therefore chooses zero targets, which 'up to one target creature' expressly permits (115.6): no counter, no Nest trigger, no token. The engine also cannot continue past this: the only creatures left are Yawgmoth, who cannot pay his own cost ('Sacrifice ANOTHER creature'), and Bats, which is not a token -- sacrificing Bats would pay Bats nothing and remove the drainer, so it is declined too.
One final Bats trigger. Opponents 28 -> 27 each; you 35 -> 34. Library 54, hand 5. No tokens remain. Bats triggers: 13. Table life removed: 52.
2 citations on the record
Step 11 of 11
ANSWER (b) and the final state-based-action check. Ledger of Bats triggers, by half. SACRIFICE half -- six tokens were sacrificed to Yawgmoth's cost: Insect A (pre-resolution, step 1), B, C, E, G, I. CREATION half -- seven tokens were created by Nest of Scarabs: C and D from the two activations that used an external/Bats sink, E and F from the single proliferate (two Nest triggers), and G, H, I from the three terminal activations. Six plus seven is THIRTEEN Bats triggers, each draining every opponent for 1 (119.3). Note what did NOT pay: Grizzly Bears' death and the three Insects killed by counters (D, F, H) are four deaths worth nothing to Bats -- but only THREE of those four are what a 'you control' death-metering drainer would have banked, because Grizzly Bears is an opponent's creature. Zulaport Cutthroat (stack 003) reads 'Whenever this creature or another creature you control dies' and Bastion of Remembrance (stack 002) reads 'Whenever a creature you control dies'; the Bears is not a creature you control, so its death does not match either ability's trigger event and neither ability triggers on it (603.2); the word 'you' on each of those objects refers to that object's controller -- you -- and not to opponent A (109.5). They bank the three counter-killed Insects D, F and H, which are tokens you control. The one drainer that would see all four is stack 004's Blood Artist, which is controller-agnostic -- 'Whenever this creature or another creature dies' carries no controller restriction, so the Bears' death does pay it (though it drains one 'target player', not each opponent). Final SBA check: every opponent is at 27, well above 0, so no player loses the game (704.5a); you are at 34 and drew only 6 cards from a 60-card library. Every Insect token that reached a graveyard, whether sacrificed or counter-killed, then ceased to exist (704.5d), which is why nine token deaths leave no trace in any graveyard.
THIRTEEN Bats triggers. Each opponent loses 13 life: 40 -> 27. Total removed from the table: 13 x 4 = 52. Opposing life 160 -> 108 (32.5% of the table). THE COMPARISON, STATED PER SIBLING, BECAUSE THE TWO SIBLING BOARDS ARE NOT THE SAME BOARD. LIKE-FOR-LIKE AGAINST STACK 003: 003 is this exact three-body board with the drainer slot filled by Zulaport Cutthroat, and its checker-passed 7 drains / 28 opposing life is a WITH-proliferate figure, exactly as this line's is -- so this is 13 drains vs 7 and 52 pod life vs 28, +24 life, 86% more. STACK 002 IS A DIFFERENT BOARD AND ITS 28 IS NOT COMPARABLE TO THIS LINE: 002 carries a fourth body, the 1/1 white Human Soldier token that Bastion of Remembrance's own enters-the-battlefield ability creates (an enchantment cannot be a body), and its headline 7 drains / 28 life is its explicit NO-proliferate board invariant while this line spends the proliferate. 002's comparable with-proliferate figure is 8 drains / 32 opposing life, against which this is 13 drains vs 8 and 52 pod life vs 32, +20 life, +62.5%. Both comparisons favour Mirkwood Bats and neither is the other; 002, 003 and this stack are three boards and must never be presented as one result on one board. The margin's reason is structural, not incidental: a creation-and-sacrifice drainer is paid twice by a single Yawgmoth activation, whereas a death drainer is paid once per body, and Bats' 2/3 body adds two free counter sinks that a 1/1 drainer cannot offer. The price is your own life total: Bats gives you nothing back, so you finish at 34 rather than the 42 that 003's Zulaport Cutthroat -- which gains you 1 life per drain -- left you at (002 ends at 42 on its no-proliferate invariant and 43 on its proliferate line). NOT LETHAL, and not close.
5 citations on the record

ANSWER (a): ONE Yawgmoth activation paid with an Insect token produces TWO Mirkwood Bats triggers, and they never sit on the stack together. Bats reads 'Whenever you create or sacrifice a token' and a single activation supplies one of each: the sacrifice is an ACTIVATION COST, paid after the ability is already the topmost object on the stack (602.2a, 602.2b -> 601.2h), so the sacrifice trigger is put on the stack ABOVE the ability (117.2a, 603.3) and resolves BEFORE it; the creation does not happen until Nest of Scarabs' own trigger resolves two resolutions later and puts a token onto the battlefield (701.7a). An activation that places no counter is worth ONE (sacrifice half only). Bats says 'a token', singular, and 603.2c settles the plural case both ways: once per trigger event, but repeatedly if one event contains multiple occurrences -- so a Nest trigger that placed N counters at once would create N tokens in one event and trigger Bats N times, though every Nest trigger in this line places exactly one counter and is worth exactly one. Counter-KILLED Insects pay nothing on the sacrifice half: 704.5f is the game putting a 0/0 into the graveyard, not the controller sacrificing it (701.21a), so those bodies pay Bats only once, when they were created. ANSWER (b): THIRTEEN Bats triggers -- six sacrifices (Insects A, B, C, E, G, I) and seven creations (C, D, E, F from the sink phase including two from a single proliferate, then G, H, I). Each opponent loses 13 life, 40 -> 27; 52 life removed from the table; 160 opposing life -> 108. MORE THAN BOTH SIBLING ENGINES -- BUT THEY MUST BE QUOTED SEPARATELY, BECAUSE ONLY 003'S BOARD IS THIS BOARD. Like-for-like against stack 003: 003 is the same three-body board with Zulaport Cutthroat in the drainer slot, and its checker-passed 7 drains / 28 opposing life is a WITH-proliferate figure, exactly as this line is -- 13 drains vs 7, 52 pod life vs 28, +24 life, 86% more. Stack 002 is NOT this board and its 28 is not comparable to this line: 002's board carries a fourth body, the 1/1 white Human Soldier token Bastion of Remembrance's own enters-the-battlefield ability creates (an enchantment cannot be a body), and its headline 7 drains / 28 life is its explicit NO-proliferate board invariant while this line spends the proliferate. 002's comparable with-proliferate figure is 8 drains / 32 opposing life; against that, 13 drains vs 8 and 52 pod life vs 32, +20 life, +62.5%. Never present 002, 003 and this stack as one result on one board. Two structural reasons, and the second is the one that would be missed: a creation-and-sacrifice drainer is paid TWICE per activation where a death drainer is paid once per body; and Mirkwood Bats' 2/3 body absorbs two -1/-1 counters and lives at 0/1 (122.1a, 704.5f), which a 1/1 drainer cannot do -- Bats' first counter slot buys one body-neutral activation, and its second slot makes the single {B}{B} proliferate worth a second Nest token, which funds the other activation. Only the first of the two is an activation's counter event, and it alone is what qualifies Bats as proliferate's second counter-bearing permanent (Bears and Bats both carrying a counter) instead of Bears alone; the second counter is placed BY the proliferate, which can only grow a permanent that already has one, so it was never a counter one of your Insects could have taken instead. The bound is still strictly FINITE, not infinite: Yawgmoth cannot target himself (protection from Humans, 702.16b), Bears absorbs two counters and Bats two, and after that every activation is net -1 body. The cost is your own life: Bats gains you nothing, so you end at 34 where 003's Zulaport Cutthroat -- which gains you 1 life per drain -- left you at 42 (002 ends at 42 on its no-proliferate invariant and 43 on its proliferate line). NOT LETHAL -- 52 of 160 opposing life.

Warren Soultrader + Gravecrawler + Bastion of Remembrance

is the drain unbounded, and what does one iteration cost?

The elegant clause is CR 113.6f: an ability that modifies which zones its own card may be cast from functions in that zone. Gravecrawler's permission is therefore live in the graveyard every iteration, its Zombie condition met by Warren Soultrader, whom “another creature” keeps permanently out of the loop. Each Treasure is an artifact and not a creature, so it funds the recast without restriction. The holding: the loop pays for itself, one drain a lap, and nothing on this board brings it to an end.

The board and the walk-through — 14 step(s)

1
Step 1

Establish the stack order before anyone passes priority. Warren Soultrader's activated ability was put on the stack first (pos 0); its costs — pay 1 life, sacrifice another creature — were then paid, and Gravecrawler's death during that cost payment triggered Bastion of Remembrance. A triggered ability does nothing at the moment it triggers; it is put on the stack the next time a player would receive priority, i.e. after activation is complete, so it lands ON TOP of the ability that caused it (pos 1).

2
Step 2

Confirm the cost payment that produced this stack was legal, and locate Gravecrawler. Gravecrawler is a creature you control other than Warren Soultrader, so it satisfies 'another creature'; sacrificing it moved it from the battlefield directly to its owner's graveyard. You had 40 life and the life component of the cost was 1, so that payment was legal.

3
Step 3

All players pass priority. The TOP object resolves first: Bastion of Remembrance's triggered ability.

4
Step 4

State-based actions are checked before any player receives priority again. No player is at 0 or less life (you 40; opponents 39 each), no creature has lethal damage, and nothing else on this board qualifies. No state-based action is performed and no ability has triggered, so the active player (you) receives priority with Warren Soultrader's ability still on the stack.

5
Step 5

All players pass again. Warren Soultrader's activated ability resolves: create a Treasure token. Establish here, once, why nothing further triggers — this is what caps the drain at exactly 1 per iteration. A triggered ability triggers only when a game event or game state MATCHES its trigger event. Bastion of Remembrance has exactly two triggered abilities: 'When this enchantment enters, create a 1/1 white Human Soldier creature token' (its entry is long past) and 'Whenever a creature you control dies, each opponent loses 1 life and you gain 1 life'. Bastion has no trigger event of the form 'a permanent enters' or 'a creature enters' at all, so creating a token — of any kind — matches neither ability. Creating the Treasure is therefore not a trigger event for Bastion, and the same rule governs steps 7 and 8 below.

6
Step 6

ITERATION 1, part (1) of the question — permission to cast Gravecrawler from the graveyard. Before you receive that priority the game checks state-based actions and performs none: with the stack empty the board is you at 40, each opponent at 39, Warren Soultrader and Bastion of Remembrance on the battlefield and the newly created Treasure on the battlefield as well, so no listed condition is met and no triggered ability is waiting to be put on the stack; the check therefore performs nothing and priority is handed back. You then receive priority with an empty stack in your precombat main phase; the phase does not end merely because the stack emptied. Gravecrawler's own static ability reads 'You may cast this card from your graveyard as long as you control a Zombie.' Two rules are needed, not one. 113.6f is the rule that makes an ability of this kind — one that modifies which zones that object can be cast from — function everywhere, including from the graveyard; without it, appealing to 604.6 alone would be circular, since the graveyard is a zone you could cast Gravecrawler from ONLY because of this very ability. 604.6 then describes the ordinary operation of such a static ability in that zone, and 601.3 is what the permission is needed for. The Zombie condition is met by Warren Soultrader, whose printed type line is Creature — Zombie Goblin Wizard; Soultrader is never sacrificed by this loop (its own ability requires sacrificing ANOTHER creature), so the permission is available on every iteration.

7
Step 7

ITERATION 1, part (1) continued — does the Treasure pay for the cast? Gravecrawler's mana cost is {B}. First establish that the Treasure's ability, '{T}, Sacrifice this token: Add one mana of any color', qualifies as a mana ability: it is an activated ability, it requires no target, it could add mana to your mana pool when it resolves, and it is not a loyalty ability — the three criteria of 605.1a, all met. Because it IS a mana ability, you may activate it while casting Gravecrawler, before costs are paid, and it does not use the stack. The Treasure is an artifact token, not a creature, so the summoning-sickness restriction — written only for a creature's activated ability with the tap symbol — does not reach it even though it was created this turn. Sacrificing it is not a creature you control dying, so no game event matches Bastion's trigger event (the rule stated at step 5) and Bastion does not trigger.

8
Step 8

All players pass. Gravecrawler, a permanent spell with no targets, resolves and enters the battlefield under your control. It is a new object with no relation to the card that was in the graveyard. Nothing dies during this cast or resolution, so no game event matches Bastion's 'Whenever a creature you control dies' trigger event, and Bastion does not trigger.

9
Step 9

You receive priority and activate Warren Soultrader's ability again. Costs: pay 1 life (you have 40, so this is payable) and sacrifice another creature — Gravecrawler, which you control and which is not Soultrader itself. Summoning sickness is irrelevant: the sacrifice is a cost of Soultrader's ability, not of any ability of Gravecrawler with a tap symbol.

10
Step 10

Gravecrawler dying is a creature you control dying, so Bastion of Remembrance triggers. The trigger is put on the stack the next time a player would receive priority — after the activation is complete — so it goes above Soultrader's ability, exactly as in step 1.

11
Step 11

All players pass; Bastion's trigger resolves.

12
Step 12

State-based actions are checked — nobody is at 0 or less — the active player receives priority, all players pass, and Warren Soultrader's ability resolves, creating a Treasure token.

13
Step 13

ANSWER TO PART (2) — the net ledger of one completed iteration, read off steps 6–12. Life paid: 1 (step 9). Life gained: 1 (step 11). Treasures created: 1 (step 12). Treasures sacrificed: 1 (step 7). Drain: 1 per opponent (step 11), and exactly one drain per iteration because the only event matching Bastion's trigger is Gravecrawler's death (steps 5, 7, 8, 10).

14
Step 14

ANSWER TO PART (1) — is the loop unbounded from this board with no outside mana or life? Every resource an iteration consumes it also replaces, in the same iteration, from inside the loop: the {B} for Gravecrawler comes from the Treasure the previous activation made, whose ability is a mana ability (steps 5, 7, 12); the 1 life for Soultrader comes back from Bastion (steps 9, 11); Gravecrawler is a card, so it returns to the graveyard each time and its own ability functions there (steps 6, 9); the Zombie that grants the graveyard permission is Warren Soultrader, which the loop never sacrifices (step 6). Nothing in the loop draws on the three tapped Swamps, the empty hand, or the library. The loop is therefore repeatable an unbounded number of times, and because it can be repeated indefinitely the shortcut rules let you declare a number of iterations rather than perform them.

Step 14 of 14
ANSWER TO PART (1) — is the loop unbounded from this board with no outside mana or life? Every resource an iteration consumes it also replaces, in the same iteration, from inside the loop: the {B} for Gravecrawler comes from the Treasure the previous activation made, whose ability is a mana ability (steps 5, 7, 12); the 1 life for Soultrader comes back from Bastion (steps 9, 11); Gravecrawler is a card, so it returns to the graveyard each time and its own ability functions there (steps 6, 9); the Zombie that grants the graveyard permission is Warren Soultrader, which the loop never sacrifices (step 6). Nothing in the loop draws on the three tapped Swamps, the empty hand, or the library. The loop is therefore repeatable an unbounded number of times, and because it can be repeated indefinitely the shortcut rules let you declare a number of iterations rather than perform them.
YES on both halves of part (1): the graveyard cast has a legal permission on every iteration, and each activation's Treasure exactly funds the next cast. The loop is self-funding and unbounded in the rules sense; in practice it terminates because the drain is not — the operative threshold is 0 life, so 40 drain events bring a 40-life opponent to exactly 0, and one drain has already resolved on the stack as given, leaving 39 further iterations after step 5 to put all four opponents at 0 simultaneously, whereupon each of them loses the game. 'Infinite lifeloss' as a containment tag is CONFIRMED for this board, with the arithmetic that matters: 1 life per opponent per iteration, 4 across the pod per iteration, 40 drain events to kill each seat.
2 citations on the record

The stack as given resolves top-down: Bastion of Remembrance's trigger first (each opponent 40 → 39, you 39 → 40), then Warren Soultrader's ability, creating one Treasure. One full further iteration — cast Gravecrawler from the graveyard for {B} paid by sacrificing that Treasure, then pay 1 life and sacrifice Gravecrawler to Soultrader — leaves each opponent at 38 and returns the board to an identical state. Both halves of part (1) are YES: Gravecrawler's own ability modifies which zone it can be cast from and so functions in the graveyard (113.6f), granting permission on every iteration with its Zombie condition met by Warren Soultrader, which the loop never sacrifices ('another creature'); and each activation's Treasure exactly funds the next {B}, its ability meeting the definition of a mana ability (605.1a) and being unrestricted because a Treasure is an artifact, not a creature. Exactly one Bastion trigger occurs per iteration: only Gravecrawler's death matches its trigger event, so neither creating nor sacrificing a Treasure adds drain (603.2). Part (2): per completed iteration your life is net 0 (dipping to 39 and refunded), each opponent is −1, and Treasures are net 0 (steady state 1). The loop is self-funding and unbounded; 40 drain events bring a seat to exactly 0 — one has already resolved, so 39 further iterations take all four opponents to 0 at once. The Commander Spellbook tag 'Infinite lifeloss' is confirmed for this exact board.

Mikaeus + Yawgmoth

does the -1/-1 counter annihilate undying's +1/+1 and re-arm the body, and what bounds the loop?

CR 704.5q is the crux — a permanent holding both a +1/+1 and a -1/-1 counter loses one of each to state-based actions. So the commander's counter does not shrink an undying body. It strips the +1/+1 that undying left behind and re-arms the creature to die again. The holding: the alternation is real, and the loop is finite, because the draw is mandatory. It needs 39 activations against the 59 the library permits, a margin of 20. One trap the record is explicit about: Viscera Seer's own sacrifice places no counter, and only the commander's counter can strip an undying return, so a mid-loop activation costs a body that cannot be re-armed.

3 citations on the record
Step 9 of 10
WHAT BOUNDS REPETITION, AND THE ONE TRAP ON THIS BOARD. First, that the line repeats at all: after step 6's state-based-action check the stack is empty, it is your precombat main phase and priority returns to you, and a player may activate an activated ability any time they have priority. Yawgmoth's ability carries no mana symbol in its cost and no activation restriction, so it may be activated again at {0} available — that is what makes steps 8-10 repetition claims rather than a one-shot. Three quantities then bound the repetition, and the one further free sacrifice outlet on this board is a TRAP, not a bonus.
(1) LIFE is not the bound: you pay 1 life per activation and Bastion returns exactly 1 life per creature death, so your total is flat at 40 and the 119.4 requirement that you have at least the life you are paying is never threatened. (2) DRAIN is the clock: one creature dies per activation, so each activation removes 1 life PER OPPONENT and 4 across the pod. From the post-resolution 39 per seat, 39 further activations put every opponent at 0 and they lose to state-based actions; that is 156 pod life removed. (3) The LIBRARY is the hard bound and it is finite: Yawgmoth's ability reads 'Put a -1/-1 counter on up to one target creature and draw a card' with no 'may', and its controller follows those instructions in the order written, so the draw is MANDATORY and cannot be declined. With 59 cards left you may activate at most 59 more times; a 60th activation would attempt a draw from an empty library and YOU lose the game at the next state-based-action check. The loop is therefore NOT infinite — a 59-activation engine that needs 39, with 20 activations of margin. (4) THE SECOND OUTLET IS A TRAP — CORRECTED FROM THE PREVIOUS PASS, WHICH CALLED IT 'STRICTLY FAVOURABLE'. Viscera Seer is back on the battlefield from step 2 and has its own activated ability, 'Sacrifice a creature: Scry 1' — an activated ability written cost-then-effect whose cost contains no mana symbol, so it is free even at {0} available, and each use kills a creature and so triggers Bastion for another 1 per seat while consuming no library. READ THE COST EXACTLY, BECAUSE THE PREVIOUS PASS DID NOT: the Seer's cost is 'Sacrifice A creature', NOT 'another creature' as Yawgmoth's cost is. The Seer may therefore eat ANY creature you control — including Mikaeus, including Yawgmoth, and including ITSELF. That distinction is what makes the finisher count 5 rather than 3. It is nevertheless ENGINE-ENDING if used mid-loop, and a pilot who reads 'free extra drain' and fires it kills their own combo. WHY: the engine's steady state is exactly one ARMED body (no +1/+1 counter, undying-eligible) and one LOADED body (one +1/+1 counter, not eligible), and only Yawgmoth's -1/-1 counter can strip a +1/+1 counter — it does so via the step-6 state-based action, one counter per activation, which is why one Yawgmoth activation re-arms exactly one body. The Seer's ability places no counter at all. So if you sacrifice the ARMED body to the Seer, undying returns it WITH a +1/+1 counter and now BOTH bodies are loaded and NEITHER is undying-eligible; and if you sacrifice the LOADED body to the Seer, its undying trigger's intervening 'if' is false and that body is gone PERMANENTLY. THE TWO BRANCHES DIFFER IN WHERE THE PERMANENT LOSS FALLS, AND THE PREVIOUS PASS'S 'either way the next Yawgmoth activation must sacrifice a counter-bearing body' IS FALSE IN THE SECOND: in branch A (armed body eaten) nothing is lost yet, both bodies carry counters, and the NEXT Yawgmoth activation must sacrifice a counter-bearing body, which dies for good; in branch B (loaded body eaten) the permanent loss has ALREADY happened and the survivor is ARMED, so the next Yawgmoth sacrifice loses nothing permanently — it returns loaded. THE CONCLUSION IS UNCHANGED, because both branches converge on the same state, ONE alternation body, and one body can never sustain the alternation. An activation kills exactly one body and yields exactly one -1/-1 counter, which strips exactly one +1/+1 counter, and it can never strip the counter on the body it just ate: that body was sacrificed to pay the cost, so if you had targeted it, on resolution it is no longer in the zone it was in when it was targeted and is an illegal target; and if you targeted anything else, its +1/+1 counter simply stays. Re-arming therefore always costs a DIFFERENT body's death, and with one alternation body left the only other creatures are Mikaeus — whose death removes undying from that body entirely, since the grant is his — and Yawgmoth, which its own 'another creature' cost may not sacrifice. The alternation is over within one or two further activations in both branches. ONE mid-loop Seer sacrifice therefore buys 1 drain per seat and costs you roughly 34 of the 39 activations you still need: you collect about five drains from the collapsing board instead of the 39 the intact alternation would have delivered. THE ONLY SAFE USE IS AS A FINISHER, after the alternation is no longer needed — and at that point at most FIVE free Seer sacrifices exist in total, CORRECTED FROM THREE, which omitted Mikaeus and Yawgmoth. The finisher board is exactly four bodies: Yawgmoth, Mikaeus, and the two alternation bodies, one armed and one loaded, one of which is the Seer itself. Order it so the SEER EATS ITSELF LAST, since its outlet leaves the battlefield with it: (1) sacrifice the ARMED body — it dies for a Bastion drain and undying returns it LOADED, no permanent loss; (2)-(4) sacrifice, in any order, the three bodies other than the Seer that are now on the battlefield — the loaded alternation body (its intervening 'if' is false, permanent), Mikaeus (his own grant reads 'OTHER non-Human creatures you control get +1/+1 and have undying', so it excludes himself and he has no undying, permanent) and Yawgmoth (a Legendary Creature — Human Cleric per cards.json, so Mikaeus's non-Human grant never reached it, permanent); (5) sacrifice the Seer to itself, permanent. Five deaths, five Bastion drains, zero library consumed. The count is the same 5 in EITHER orientation of which alternation body is armed: if the Seer is the armed body, it is step (1) and it comes straight back to keep working. SIX IS IMPOSSIBLE: four bodies plus exactly ONE undying return, because only one body is armed at a time and neither Yawgmoth (Human) nor Mikaeus (excluded by his own 'Other') can undy. ACCOUNTING CONSEQUENCE: margin = 59 - (39 - k) = 20 + k, where k is the number of finisher Seer sacrifices taken and 0 <= k <= 5. The margin is 20 activations clean and at most 25 with all five finisher sacrifices — a bounded range, NOT an open-ended lower bound as an earlier pass asserted, and NOT the 20-23 the previous pass computed from a count of three. Nothing else on this board caps the loop: the loop costs no mana, Gravecrawler cannot be recast from the graveyard because that requires {B} and mana available is {0}, and Mikaeus need only remain on the battlefield, which it does because Yawgmoth's cost sacrifices 'another creature' and you always choose one of the two designated bodies.
10 citations on the record
Step 10 of 10
HOW THE KILL BECOMES A WIN. The 39th further activation brings every opponent from 1 to 0 life; the drain is one simultaneous event per Bastion resolution, so all four seats reach 0 together and all four lose the game at the next state-based-action check. You are still in the game at that moment: your life is flat at 40 (step 9, item 1), and you have used 39 of the 59 draws the library permits, so the deck-out state-based action has not been met.
With all four of your opponents having left the game and you still in it, you win the game immediately. This is the rule that converts four simultaneous losses in a five-player pod into a win; the loss of the opponents alone does not state it. The Mikaeus + Yawgmoth line is a verified kill on this board.
1 citation on the record

The stack fully resolves. Undying returns Viscera Seer (its condition read off the creature as it last existed on the battlefield, where it had no counters) with a +1/+1 counter, so it is 3/3. Bastion drains 1 life PER OPPONENT (4 seats x 1 = 4 across the pod, 160 -> 156) and gains you 1, putting you back to 40. Yawgmoth's ability then resolves on its still-legal target: Gravecrawler takes a -1/-1 counter and you draw. THE CRUX: the next state-based-action check removes one +1/+1 and one -1/-1 counter from Gravecrawler simultaneously (704.5q), leaving it with ZERO counters at 3/2 and alive — so YES, Gravecrawler is eligible for undying the next time it dies. The -1/-1 counter is not a shrink, it is a RESET. The same check is verified on the other body: Viscera Seer, printed 1/1, is 2/2 when it holds both counters, so toughness 2 clears 704.5f and it survives to be re-armed in its turn — the alternation holds in both directions. Repetition is legal: priority returns to you with the stack empty and a player may activate an activated ability any time they have priority (117.1b), and the ability needs no mana. The loop is FINITE: life is flat (pay 1, Bastion returns 1), the drain clock needs 39 more activations to zero all four opponents, and the draw is mandatory (608.2c — no 'may' on the card), capping the engine at 59 more activations before you deck yourself. THE TRAP, corrected from the previous pass: Viscera Seer's own free 'Sacrifice a creature: Scry 1' outlet is NOT a strictly favourable bonus. It places no counter, and only Yawgmoth's -1/-1 counter can strip the +1/+1 counter an undying return leaves behind, one per activation — so a mid-loop Seer sacrifice either loads BOTH bodies (sac the armed one; 702.93a returns it with a counter) or permanently loses one (sac the loaded one; undying's intervening 'if' fails). The two branches differ in WHERE the permanent loss falls, and the previous pass's 'either way' sentence was wrong about it: in branch A (armed body eaten) nothing is lost yet and the NEXT Yawgmoth sacrifice is the one that loses a body for good; in branch B (loaded body eaten) the loss has ALREADY happened and the survivor is armed, so the next Yawgmoth sacrifice loses nothing permanently. The conclusion is unchanged because both branches converge on ONE alternation body, and one body can never be re-armed: an activation's single -1/-1 counter can never land on the body that same activation sacrificed to pay its cost — that body is no longer in the zone it was in when it was targeted, so it is an illegal target (608.2b) — and re-arming therefore always costs a different body's death, which with one body left means eating Mikaeus (who is the source of undying) or nothing. Fire the Seer mid-loop and you trade 1 drain per seat for roughly 34 of the 39 activations you need. Use it ONLY as a finisher, where at most FIVE such sacrifices exist — CORRECTED from three: the Seer's cost is 'Sacrifice a creature', NOT 'another creature', so it also eats Mikaeus (whose 'Other non-Human' grant excludes himself) and Yawgmoth (a Human Cleric, whom that grant never reached), and finally itself; four bodies plus the one available undying return is 5, and six is impossible. Margin is therefore 20 activations clean and at most 25 with all five finisher sacrifices — a bounded range, not an open lower bound. All four opponents reach 0 together and leave the game; you are still in it, so you win (104.2a). VERDICT on the deck's declared second kill: the Mikaeus + Yawgmoth line is REAL and lethal on this board, but it is a library-bounded loop, not an infinite combo. A containment record is not a verification; this resolves one of the nineteen and it holds with 20 activations of margin (20-25 depending on finisher Seer sacrifices).

Gravecrawler + Phyrexian Altar + Carrion Feeder + Bastion of Remembrance

is the drain unbounded and does it cost anything?

One line of rules text carries the whole thing. Phyrexian Altar's ability needs no target and could add mana, so it is a mana ability (CR 605.1a) and never uses the stack (CR 605.3b) — the {B} is in the pool before anyone receives priority, and there is nothing to respond to. Sacrificing Gravecrawler refunds exactly the mana that recasts him, with Carrion Feeder holding the Zombie condition and never being the sacrifice. The holding: unbounded, and free — each lap costs nothing and gains you a life.

Step 1

Establish what is on the stack, and check it against the corrected scenario. Phyrexian Altar's ability — 'Sacrifice a creature: Add one mana of any color.' — requires no target, could add mana when it resolves, and is not a loyalty ability, so it is a mana ability. A mana ability never uses the stack: it resolved immediately when it was activated and put {B} into your mana pool, where it stays as unspent mana until the end of the step or phase. Bastion of Remembrance's ability triggered on Gravecrawler's death, and that death happened DURING the payment of the Altar's activation cost — i.e. while an ability was being activated, which is expressly a time at which an ability may trigger. Nothing happened at that moment; the trigger was held and put on the stack the next time a player would receive priority.

Its condition is checked now: you control Carrion Feeder, whose type line is Creature — Zombie. Exquisite Blood + South Wind Avatar

the loop is mandatory — does it kill the pod or draw the game?

Counsel's favourite kind of question: the loop is mandatory — neither card says “may” — so does the game simply end in a draw? No, and CR 732.1b is why. A draw requires a sequence that could be repeated indefinitely, and this one cannot, because every cycle takes a life from every opponent. There is a way to stop, which is all CR 104.4b asks. The holding: drain 40 takes all four opponents to 0, state-based actions are checked before the resulting triggers reach the stack, and they leave together (CR 704.5a). You are still there, so you win (CR 104.2a).

The board and the walk-through — 12 step(s)
The board
YouCreatures South Wind Avatar, Carrion FeederPermanents Exquisite BloodLands Swamp, SwampAlready paid Insect token XMana {0} (both Swamps are tapped and nothing else produces mana)
Opponent 1 — 40 lifeno permanents
Opponent 2 — 40 lifeno permanents
Opponent 3 — 40 lifeno permanents
Opponent 4 — 40 lifeno permanents
On the stack, top first
  1. South Wind Avatar's triggered ability — 'Whenever another creature you control dies, you gain life equal to its toughness.' Triggered on Insect token X's death during cost payment. Insect token X had toughness 1.
    controlled by you
    — resolves first
  2. Carrion Feeder's activated ability — 'Sacrifice a creature: Put a +1/+1 counter on this creature.' Cost already paid: Insect token X sacrificed.
    controlled by you
1
Step 1

Starting position. Carrion Feeder's activated ability is on the stack at pos 0 (bottom). Activation put the ability on the stack FIRST, as the topmost object at that moment; the activation cost (sacrifice a creature) was then paid as part of the same activation process, which is why Insect token X is NOT on the battlefield. Your bodies are South Wind Avatar and Carrion Feeder (2 creatures). Exquisite Blood is your only other permanent. Opponents: 4 seats at 40 life each (per-seat 40; pod total 160). You are at 40.

2
Step 2

Insect token X died on the battlefield during cost payment, so South Wind Avatar's first ability ('Whenever another creature you control dies, you gain life equal to its toughness') triggered at that moment. The next time a player would receive priority, the game FIRST performs state-based actions and only THEN puts waiting triggered abilities on the stack (117.5) -- that ordering is what makes the following two things happen in this order. State-based action: the token is now in the graveyard, a zone other than the battlefield, so it ceases to exist. Then the already-triggered death trigger is put on the stack above the Carrion Feeder ability, so it is at pos 1 (top) and resolves first.

3
Step 3

Every opponent passes priority (scenario assumption: 'No player responds to anything; every opponent passes priority throughout'). All players having passed in succession, the top object resolves: the death trigger. It requires information from a specific object -- the dead Insect token -- which is no longer in the zone it was expected to be in (it ceased to exist at step 2), so the effect uses that object's LAST KNOWN INFORMATION: toughness 1. You therefore gain 1 life. This same pass-and-resolve mechanism drives every one of the roughly eighty resolutions in steps 5 through 10 below; it is not restated at each one.

4
Step 4

That life gain is a life-gain event for you, so South Wind Avatar's SECOND ability ('Whenever you gain life, each opponent loses 1 life') triggers once. It is put on the stack above the Carrion Feeder ability. Note the direction: this ability watches YOU gaining life, not opponents losing it.

5
Step 5

All players pass again (step 3) and drain #1 resolves: each opponent loses 1 life. This is one effect hitting all four seats at once.

6
Step 6

Exquisite Blood ('Whenever an opponent loses life, you gain that much life') sees four opponents losing life. That one happening contains four occurrences of its trigger event -- one per opponent -- so it triggers four times, each for 1 life. Note the direction: Exquisite Blood watches an OPPONENT losing life, not you gaining it. Because these four have triggered simultaneously, they are placed on the stack by their controller, in APNAP order, in any order that player chooses; you control all four and they are identical (each gains you 1 life), so the ordering choice changes nothing.

7
Step 7

The loop's shape. Resolving ONE Exquisite Blood trigger gains you 1 life; that life gain triggers South Wind Avatar's second ability, which is put on the stack ON TOP of the remaining Exquisite Blood triggers and therefore resolves next. So the sequence alternates strictly: one Exquisite Blood resolution, then one drain, then one Exquisite Blood resolution, then one drain. Each cycle nets +3 unresolved Exquisite Blood triggers on the stack, and neither card says 'may', so no player may decline any of it.

8
Step 8

The loop is mandatory but it is NOT a draw, and the limiter has a name. A 'loop' in the rules is a state in which a set of actions could be repeated INDEFINITELY, and the draw rule applies only to a loop 'repeating a sequence of events with no way to stop'. Neither condition is met here: every iteration changes the game state irreversibly -- each drain lowers every opponent's life by 1, adjusting four life totals that never rise again -- so the sequence cannot be repeated indefinitely and there IS a way to stop. It terminates after a finite, computable number of iterations when a state-based action ends the game. 732.4's bare 'only mandatory actions' sentence therefore does not reach this sequence, both because it imports 104.4b by its own cross-reference and because 732.1b's definition of a loop is unsatisfied.

9
Step 9

Count the iterations. Opponents start at 40 and each drain removes exactly 1 from each seat, so drain #40 is the one that brings all four seats to 0 simultaneously. Drain #1 followed the death trigger's life gain; every later drain #k (k >= 2) is preceded by exactly one Exquisite Blood resolution, so 39 Exquisite Blood triggers resolve before drain #40.

10
Step 10

Drain #40 finishes resolving and a player would receive priority. State-based actions are checked FIRST, before the four new Exquisite Blood triggers from that drain are put on the stack (704.3, the same ordering cited at step 2). All four opponents have 0 life, so all four lose the game as a single simultaneous state-based action; because state-based actions were performed, the check repeats, and the waiting triggers never get put on the stack.

11
Step 11

Each opponent that lost the game leaves the game. You are the only player left, so you win immediately and the game ends immediately. Nothing still on the stack resolves.

12
Step 12

Robustness note, and the one figure that is reading-dependent. Even if one insisted Exquisite Blood saw the drain as a single event and triggered only once, the alternation in step 7 is unchanged -- one Exquisite Blood resolution between successive drains -- so the drain count (40), the final per-seat total (0) and the pod total (0) are identical under every reading. YOUR final life is not: under the four-triggers reading and under a once-for-1 reading it is 80, but under a once-for-the-full-4-life-lost reading it would be 40 + 1 + 39x4 = 197. The kill does not depend on the reading; your life total does.

The undying pair

Butcher Ghoul + Geralf's Messenger under Yawgmoth — is the loop unbounded, and what does each opponent lose per cycle?

The clause that decides this case is one the deck usually loves: "and draw a card" — no "may" anywhere in it, and a resolving ability's instructions must be followed (CR 608.2c). The alternation itself is genuine and elegant: each undying return leaves a +1/+1 counter, the commander's -1/-1 annihilates it by state-based action (CR 704.5q), and the body dies counter-free again, forever in principle. But "unbounded" is refuted on the record, because every activation is a mandatory draw against a library of roughly 55, and the draw after the last one loses the game (CR 121.4, 704.5b). The holding: library-bounded at ~55 activations — and the bound never saves the table, because each two-activation cycle takes 2 from every opponent and 4 from the Messenger's target at net-zero life to the pilot, so ~30 activations end the pod with ~25 draws of margin.

The board and the walk-through — 9 step(s)
