Resolve
Step 1 of 9
Resolve the top object: Zulaport Cutthroat's triggered ability (Geralf's Messenger, a creature you control, died).
Each of the three opponents loses 1 life (40 -> 39, 40 -> 39, 40 -> 39) and you gain 1 life (20 -> 21; the 1 life for Yawgmoth's activation cost was already paid, per the scenario's stated life totals). Pod total so far: 3 life.
2 citations on the record
Step 2 of 9
Resolve the next object: Geralf's Messenger's undying trigger. Check its intervening 'if' clause on resolution.
The condition — it had no +1/+1 counters on it when it died — was true at the trigger event and remains true on resolution, so the ability resolves: Geralf's Messenger returns from your graveyard to the battlefield under your control with a +1/+1 counter on it. Its 'enters tapped' replacement effect applies, so it enters tapped. Its enters-the-battlefield ability triggers: 'When this creature enters, target opponent loses 2 life.'
4 citations on the record
Step 3 of 9
Before any player receives priority, the Messenger's enters trigger is put on the stack — above Yawgmoth's still-pending activated ability. You choose a target: opponent 1.
The stack is now (top to bottom): Messenger's ETB trigger targeting opponent 1; Yawgmoth's activated ability targeting Butcher Ghoul.
2 citations on the record
Step 4 of 9
Resolve Geralf's Messenger's enters-the-battlefield trigger.
Opponent 1 loses 2 life (39 -> 37). Opponents now at 37 / 39 / 39.
2 citations on the record
Step 5 of 9
Resolve Yawgmoth, Thran Physician's activated ability (bottom of the original stack). Target check first.
Butcher Ghoul is still on the battlefield and is a legal target. A -1/-1 counter is put on it — it now has one +1/+1 counter and one -1/-1 counter — and you draw a card. The draw is mandatory ('and draw a card', no 'may'). Library ~55 -> ~54.
2 citations on the record
Step 6 of 9
State-based actions are checked before any player receives priority: counter annihilation on Butcher Ghoul.
N=1: one +1/+1 counter and one -1/-1 counter are removed from Butcher Ghoul. It is a 1/1 with NO counters — exactly the state undying needs for its next death. No player is at 0 or less life; no creature has lethal damage or 0 toughness. The stack is empty. Board: Yawgmoth, Butcher Ghoul (1/1, counter-free), Zulaport Cutthroat, Geralf's Messenger (tapped, one +1/+1 counter). You 21; opponents 37/39/39.
2 citations on the record
Step 7 of 9
LOOP ANALYSIS — the mirrored second activation. Pay 1 life, sacrifice Butcher Ghoul (now counter-free), targeting Geralf's Messenger with the -1/-1 counter.
Butcher Ghoul died with no +1/+1 counters, so undying triggers and returns it with a +1/+1 counter; Zulaport Cutthroat triggers again (each opponent loses 1, you gain 1); the -1/-1 counter annihilates the Messenger's +1/+1 counter via the same state-based action, leaving the Messenger counter-free for the next Messenger-sacrifice; you draw a second card. The counter-stripping is what keeps the loop alive: a creature that dies WITH a +1/+1 counter fails undying's intervening 'if' and never triggers at all. Butcher Ghoul has no enters trigger, so the Ghoul half of the cycle drains only via Zulaport. PER FULL TWO-ACTIVATION CYCLE: each opponent loses 2 (Zulaport, one per death); the opponent targeted by the Messenger's enters trigger loses 2 more (so 4 total for that seat); pod total 8 (6 Zulaport + 2 Messenger). You pay 2 life and gain 2 from Zulaport — net 0 — and draw 2 cards.
3 citations on the record
Step 8 of 9
LOOP BOUND — verify or refute 'unbounded'.
'Unbounded' is REFUTED. Yawgmoth's draw is not optional: the resolving ability's instructions must be followed, and 'and draw a card' contains no 'may'. Every activation therefore consumes one library card. With ~55 cards in the library, the pilot can make at most ~55 activations (including the one just resolved) — about 27 full two-activation cycles — before the library is empty; the next mandatory draw after that would be from an empty library, and the pilot loses the game as a state-based action. The loop is BOUNDED BY LIBRARY SIZE, not by life (net 0 per cycle) or by bodies (the two undying creatures recycle indefinitely in principle). It is also not a rules-mandated loop: each iteration begins with a discretionary activation, so the pilot simply stops before the last safe draw — but a set of actions that cannot be 'repeated indefinitely' is, by the CR's own definition of a loop, not unbounded.
4 citations on the record
Step 9 of 9
LETHALITY WITHIN THE BOUND — does KILL F still kill?
Yes, comfortably. Over n activations each opponent loses n life to Zulaport (one death per activation) plus 2 per Messenger enters-trigger aimed at them; the Messenger trigger fires on every odd-numbered activation (ceil(n/2) times). At n = 30 activations (15 cycles), Zulaport alone deals 30 to each seat and the 15 Messenger triggers, spread 5/5/5, add 10 more — 40 per opponent, exactly lethal from 40, with each opponent losing the game as a state-based action at 0 or less life. 30 activations cost 30 draws against a ~55-card library, leaving ~25 cards of margin. Correct label for KILL F: not 'unbounded' but 'library-bounded (~55 iterations available), lethal to the pod in ~30'.
1 citation on the record

Stack empty after the presented stack resolves: opponents at 37/39/39, you at 21, library ~54, board Yawgmoth + Butcher Ghoul (1/1, counter-free) + Zulaport Cutthroat + Geralf's Messenger (tapped, one +1/+1 counter) — the loop is primed for the mirrored Ghoul-sacrifice activation. VERDICT: 'unbounded' is the wrong word. The loop is bounded by the library, because Yawgmoth's draw is mandatory: ~55 activations maximum (~27 full cycles), after which the next mandatory draw loses the game (121.4 / 704.5b). Per full two-activation cycle: each opponent loses 2 (Zulaport), the Messenger-targeted opponent loses 4, pod total 8, you net 0 life and draw 2. The bound does not save the table: ~30 activations kill all three opponents from 40, ~25 draws inside the bound.

At the Table

Who turns on you, when, and how the matchups go. ★ COACHING

They plan to kill the doctor. Let them — it is half right.

Three verified kills never need him on board (009, 011, 012); three do — Nest (002–004) and both library-bounded lines (010, 014). Trade him cheerfully in the grind; he recasts for two. On the engine turn spend one of your only three saves: Lightning Greaves, Supernatural Stamina, Malakir Rebirth, never a value creature.

He lands turn four in 71.9% of goldfished games, turn two in 7.2%: keep the hand with bodies, not the one promising Sol Ring, Ancient Tomb or Dark Ritual (43.6% by turn ten). By turn ten: drain 82.3%, fodder 82.3%, outlet 78.5%, ignition 73.8%, the kill itself 13.5%.

Wear the value seat and dig: his ability costs no mana, so sacrifice at instant speed on their turns, with Ophiomancer and Pawn of Ulamog paying him. Feed Gravecrawler only with a second Zombie out — your only Zombie is half of both unbounded kills. Five tutors take the primary from 7.5% to 38.9% by turn six, so each is a kill half; fetch Nest of Scarabs.

The primary pays in installments: five to eight activations at near two drains a body (002, 005), plus two more per two-toughness attacker you shrink (002, 003). Count the library first — his draw has no “may”. Mikaeus needs 39 activations against 59 (010); Butcher Ghoul plus Geralf's Messenger has 55 and kills near 30 at 8 pod life a cycle, free (014). Announce with the number.

The table flips on your SECOND noncreature payoff — Exquisite Blood, Phyrexian Altar beside Gravecrawler, Mikaeus untapping. So: cheap halves early, Nykthos held, reveal at the end step before your turn, open mana counted. Then go — 009 funds its next cast, 011 ends you at 80, 014 nets zero. Never claim you have nothing; quiet seats get hit. Claim the swarm, the drains, the edicts.

You do not fear a wrath — the drains bill for it. You fear two-mana artifacts: Grafdigger's Cage or Cursed Totem covers four of six kills, and nothing in the 99 destroys an artifact — Feed the Swarm stops at creatures and enchantments. So buy the break: bank green and white early with your removal and Grave Pact's edicts, overpay for their Disenchant the day a Cage lands, keep the hate seat's life low. If it sticks, tutor for Nest (fresh tokens) or Exquisite Blood (no graveyard).

At Rule 0, say it straight: floor 4, four Game Changers, eleven infinite records, and every kill ends all three opponents at once; you never present lethal to one seat. Then go: which shape, what is open, how deep the library?

Re-assign your seat when the board turns. Fair decks: you own the long game. A lock or a faster clock: you are the beatdown, and value play loses.

FAIR, REMOVAL-HEAVY PODS are your best table. Grind: sacrifice on end steps, draw off every body, counters on their creatures, Grave Pact billing the room. The machine arrives on its own at 4.239 bodies by turn six — buy the missing piece. Trade Yawgmoth smiling, recast him; Lightning Greaves, Supernatural Stamina and Malakir Rebirth wait for the engine turn.

SWEEPER CONTROL holds a wrath with your name on it. Invite it. Bastion of Remembrance, Zulaport Cutthroat and The Meathook Massacre bill the table as your creatures die, Blood Artist on any death; Reassembling Skeleton, Gravecrawler, Bitterblossom and Ophiomancer rebuild free. Hold two mana against a control seat on four-plus and turn the wrath into Plumb the Forbidden. Bastion sits in five verified boards and your saves cover creatures only, so hold the noncreature half.

STAX splits. Under TAXES you are the best seat: his ability costs no mana and every loop self-funds (009's Treasure, 011's refund, 012 free, 014 net zero), and Jet Medallion pays the first tax — deploy every piece the turn you can. Under LOCK PERMANENTS you are the worst: no artifact answer in the 99, so race and buy the break. Cursed Totem darkens him and your creature outlets, leaving Ashnod's Altar, Phyrexian Altar and Blasting Station, unsettled by any verified line.

AGGRO is feeding you. Black Sun's Zenith, Soul Snuffers and The Meathook Massacre all ignite Nest of Scarabs going down, so cast the wipe that leaves you a board. Life is cheap early: run Necropotence, Black Market Connections and City of Brass hot, then defend a turn early.

COMBO is your worst seat — play it with your chest. Deadly Rollick, free while he is out, and Fatal Push only hit creatures, so spend them on the enabler the turn it lands; held answers rot. Then race with Exquisite Blood: mean turn 5.085, 42.6% with a tutor, closing at instant speed. Line up the first lifegain: 012 runs on South Wind Avatar's death trigger, and Marauding Blight-Priest and Starscape Cleric gain none.

GRAVEYARD HATE halves your kills: both Gravecrawler loops and both undying lines route through the yard; Nest runs on fresh tokens and Exquisite Blood touches none. Nothing verified settles it, so under hate drop recursion and tutor Nest (38.9% by turn six) or Exquisite Blood. Bojuka Bog hates back: save it for a reanimation target.

Play

Real spots, and what you would do. Answers on click. ★ COACHING

Nobody here is frightened of you, and nothing you are holding kills anybody. The machine is running and the ceiling is missing. So what do the six mana buy — the card that makes you the table's police, the loud half of your best kill, the quiet half of your fastest one, or nothing at all while you dig five cards deep?

Nobody here is frightened of you, and nothing you are holding kills anybody. The machine is running and the ceiling is missing. So what do the six mana buy — the card that makes you the table's police, the loud half of your best kill, the quiet half of your fastest one, or nothing at all while you dig five cards deep?

Cast Grave Pact and become the table's police

Enormous, and unmistakable. Forced sacrifices are the effect this format resents most, and three rounds of them inside one rotation says your engine converts into other people's permanents on demand.

The card is excellent and this is the wrong turn for it.

Start with what it does not do. It advances no kill. Your problem tonight is not the board — the aggro seat is losing a race it started — your problem is that no configuration is on the table.

Then look at who gets paid. The control seat owns no creatures, so every edict you fire is free value for the one deck that beats you.

And price the attention. Nobody has spent removal on you yet, which is the most valuable thing you own. Three rounds of forced sacrifices is how a pod picks an archenemy, and it spends the deflection budget you will want on the turn you finally go.

Grave Pact is a keeper for the turn an opposing board is genuinely killing you, or the turn it converts a kill you already hold. Play it then and it wins a game. Play it now and you are buying protection you already have.

Cast Exquisite Blood and commit the loud half of your best kill

The most recognisable card in your deck, cast on a turn where it removes nobody's life. Everyone here has seen it before and everyone knows what it is waiting for. The control seat gets a full rotation, and every land it has been holding, to decide what to do about that.

This is your best kill and I am not talking you out of it. I am talking you out of leading with it.

The Exquisite Blood shape assembles in 6.9% of goldfished games by turn ten unassisted and 42.5% with a tutor, at a mean turn of 5.301 — the highest rate and the fastest clock of your four kills. Point every search you draw at that pair.

But five of your six mana buys a permanent that does nothing until a second card arrives, and no tutor has arrived: you are in the 39% of games that never sees one by turn ten. So it sits through a whole rotation with your cover gone, and the only work it does is tell three players what to hold.

One caution about your own deck. The checker resolved this loop on South Wind Avatar, whose own death trigger supplies the life that starts it; Marauding Blight-Priest and Starscape Cleric carry the identical drain text and gain no life, and Bastion covers that gap unresolved. Treat the Avatar as the sure thing and those two as candidates.

Cast this the turn the body is already down, or the turn you can cast both. Never as a five-mana billboard.

Cast Warren Soultrader and keep three mana up

A 3/3 for three on a board that already has five bodies. It reads as a fair creature and a chump blocker. Nobody at this table has ever lost to a Warren Soultrader, and nobody is spending an exile effect on one.

This is the play, and the reason is arithmetic rather than taste.

Count what each option leaves you needing. Grave Pact: an entire kill. Exquisite Blood: one of three bodies. Soultrader: Gravecrawler and nothing else, because the drain half is already down.

That is the kill you want to be one card away from. Each sacrifice makes the Treasure that pays for the next Gravecrawler, each iteration costs a life and hands it back, and every seat reaches zero at the same moment. It is the attempt you can make into open mana and still have a deck if it fails.

The part most pilots give away: your commander charges no mana, so the two Faerie activations are free in every branch here — you already own the digging. The three mana buy an instant-speed Skeleton return, and you spend it at the end step before your own turn, when their chances to respond are gone.

Point the counters at the Rakdos attackers, never your own bodies; their 2/1 dies to an activation you were making anyway. And keep this quiet: Soultrader makes Treasures, so the chump blocker is also the engine mana your loops run on.

Cast nothing and dig five cards deep

Every life total at the table moves five points in one turn while your board drops from five bodies to two. That is the loudest thing this deck can do without killing anybody, and it reclassifies you from the value player to the drain player in one sitting.

The instinct is right and the price is wrong.

Digging is what this deck does. Your commander turns a spare body into a card for no mana, and under Bastion of Remembrance the life you pay comes straight back, so the draw engine is genuinely free. Run it every single turn.

But look at what the six mana actually bought. Two of those five cards were free and the other three cost two mana each. You paid your whole turn for three cards and deployed nothing.

Then count what you fed it. Reassembling Skeleton is renewable fodder and one of the few bodies that comes back forever — recycling it three times in one turn is the most expensive way to buy cards you already own.

And you handed your blockers to an aggro seat with two attackers pointed somewhere.

Take the free half, refuse the expensive half. Eat the two Faeries for two cards, and if nothing on the other side of the table is worth shrinking, choose no target at all — the draw does not depend on one. Then spend the mana on a permanent.

Take: Cast Warren Soultrader and keep three mana up

You cannot destroy an artifact — no card in the 99 does. Do you spend a real political favour buying an answer, commit your hand to the one kill the Cage cannot touch, hand the problem to the seat it hurts more, or say nothing at all and keep grinding?

You cannot destroy an artifact — no card in the 99 does. Do you spend a real political favour buying an answer, commit your hand to the one kill the Cage cannot touch, hand the problem to the seat it hurts more, or say nothing at all and keep grinding?

Buy the break — pay the Golgari seat to destroy the Cage

The most information you can give away in one sentence. Three players now know which single permanent switches your deck off, and one of them owns it.

Before you spend a favour, price what you are buying back.

The Cage takes your three least-likely kills and leaves your most likely one alone. The Soultrader loop assembles in 1.1% of goldfished games unassisted and 18.0% with a tutor; the Phyrexian Altar loop 0.5% and 12.3%; the Mikaeus loop 4.6% and 33.7%. The Exquisite Blood loop is 6.9% and 42.5%, and the Cage does not see it.

So you would pay a real favour, plus your cover, to unlock the three routes you assemble least.

The damage is broad. Nothing that returns a creature from your graveyard to the battlefield works — that is your recursion, your reanimation, and every undying body you own, because undying is a return from the graveyard like any other.

Broad is not fatal. Your commander still draws, Bastion of Remembrance still drains, Bitterblossom still makes a body every upkeep, and the kill you are most likely to assemble is untouched.

Save the favour. You will want a genuine one later, and a table only lets you overpay once.

Cast Exquisite Blood now and buy the body at the end step

The most recognisable card in your deck, cast into a table that has just watched a one-mana artifact turn you off. Read that as cover rather than exposure — the seat that played the Cage believes it solved you, and this is the one thing it did not solve.

This is the play, and the reason is which half can afford to sit.

Three seats at this table can kill a creature. One seat can kill an enchantment. So the enchantment goes down early and the body never spends a turn on the table at all.

Order matters more than the cards do. Cast the Avatar last and you activate your commander the same turn it lands — his ability costs no mana and you may use it whenever you hold priority, so the pair exists for seconds instead of a rotation. It is a full turn faster, too: buying the body first means casting a 3/4 and asking it to survive three seats before the five-mana half arrives.

Keep one Swamp up when you tap out. That single black is your tutor, and casting it on the last end step before you untap means their answers are already spent when you take your turn.

And know what you are fetching: the checker resolved this loop on South Wind Avatar, whose own death trigger supplies the life. Marauding Blight-Priest and Starscape Cleric carry the same text and cost less, but nobody has resolved them, and this is not the game to find out.

Hide the famous card and put the body down first

Almost nothing. A 3/4 deathtouch body on a board full of small ones reads as defence, and it will be the least interesting permanent anybody plays this rotation.

The right instinct, applied to the wrong card.

Hiding the loud half is usually correct in this deck and I would tell you to do it with almost any other pair. It is wrong here because of who holds what.

Creature removal is everywhere at this table. Enchantment removal is one seat, probably once. So the body is the fragile half and the enchantment is the durable one, which is the reverse of how the two cards feel in your hand.

There is a tempo cost stacked on top. This line spends a turn casting a 3/4 and then needs five more mana on the turn after, so you are asking to survive two windows where the other order asks for one.

And the blocker is worth less than it looks. You already have a commander who eats attackers for free by putting counters on them, and you are not short of bodies.

Keep the shape and fix the order. Same two cards, same tutor, one turn sooner and one fewer chance for anybody to act.

Say nothing to the Golgari seat and let the reanimator pay for it

Almost nothing about your deck, and one useful sentence about somebody else's. The table learns the Cage is a problem without learning it is your problem.

This is the cheapest correct sentence available and you should say it in every branch on this page. It simply is not a plan.

The trouble is what the turn does. You spend six mana on a removal spell and two free draws, and you finish exactly as far from a kill as you started.

Waiting also idles the thing you are best at. A tutor takes the Exquisite Blood shape from 6.9% to 42.5%, only 60.6% of games see one by turn ten, and yours is in your hand doing nothing.

And Player 4 may simply not hold an artifact answer. You would be spending a rotation on somebody else's draw step and calling it a plan.

Say the sentence. Then spend the turn on the kill the Cage cannot touch.

Take: Cast Exquisite Blood now and buy the body at the end step

What the goldfish does not model (10)
  • Simulates resource development, not full games (no interaction, no removal).
  • Draw every turn including turn 1 (multiplayer Commander).
  • Mulligan: keep 7-card hands with 2-5 lands; up to 2 fresh redraws, keep the last.
  • One land drop per turn when available.
  • Mana rocks ('{T}: Add') contribute from the turn after they are cast.
  • Commander cast on first affordable turn (highest spending priority).
  • Bodies count = creatures cast + tokens parsed from 'create ... token' text.
  • Target assembly counts cards DRAWN by a turn (cast cards still count).
  • Unrestricted tutors ('search your library for a card') are modeled as wildcards: a tutor that has been drawn and is affordable fills ONE missing any_of group. Consumed once, mana paid, and a tutor that puts the card on top of the library costs a turn. Reported as the *_assisted figures; the unassisted figures beside them exclude tutors entirely.
  • Cost reducers, rituals, and extra card draw are not modeled (conservative).

The Record

The proof, one row per case. Unabridged on click. ✓ RULES-VERIFIED

Every ✓ claim on this page, with the rule text behind it. Nothing is paraphrased. Open a case for its full record.

A-002 Yawgmoth + Nest of Scarabs + Bastion of Remembrance ✓ cleared, 3 cycle(s) · 11 steps · 32 citations
Rules version June 19, 2026
The question put to the record
Resolve the stack, then run the engine as far as the rules allow. (a) Is this loop unbounded? Name precisely what Yawgmoth's ability can legally target on each iteration, given that Nest of Scarabs' replacement Insect does not exist when the target is chosen, and state the exact number of completed activations from this board and what ends it. (b) What is the net change to your life total per completed activation, and how much total life do all four opponents lose?
  1. Fix the reading of the board before resolving anything, then account for what already happened during the activation itself. THE READING: the scenario's board line for Insect token A is annotated 'already sacrificed to pay the cost of the ability now on the stack', which matches stack[0] ('1 life paid (you are at 39) and Insect token A sacrificed') and the stated life total of 39. Insect A is therefore in the graveyard, not on the battlefield, and the creatures available to pay future sacrifice costs are Insect token B and the Human Soldier token - a THREE-body board counting the one already spent (A, B, Soldier), not four. Every figure below follows from that reading. Within the activation itself the order was: Yawgmoth's ability was put on the stack, its target (Grizzly Bears) was chosen, and only then were its costs paid - 1 life (you 40 -> 39) and the sacrifice of Insect token A.
    Insect token A was a creature you controlled that died, so Bastion of Remembrance's ability ('Whenever a creature you control dies, each opponent loses 1 life and you gain 1 life') has triggered. This is a leaves-the-battlefield trigger and it looks back in time: the game consults the game state immediately before the sacrifice to see that the dying Insect was a creature YOU controlled, which is what licenses this trigger and the six that follow it - SEVEN Bastion triggers in all across the five completed activations. It triggered during the process of activating an ability, so it did nothing at that moment; it waits and is put on the stack the next time a player would receive priority, which is immediately after the activation is complete. It goes on top of Yawgmoth's ability. That the sacrificed creature was a TOKEN does not suppress this: a token that changes zones triggers applicable triggered abilities before it ceases to exist - the point that carries every one of the seven drains in this line, since all seven creatures of yours that die here are tokens. Stack, bottom to top: [0] Yawgmoth's ability targeting Grizzly Bears, [1] Bastion's drain trigger.
    CR 602.2b — “The remainder of the process for activating an ability is identical to the process for casting a spell listed in rules 601.2b–i.”
    CR 701.21a — “To sacrifice a permanent, its controller moves it from the battlefield directly to its owner’s graveyard. A player can’t sacrifice something that isn’t a permanent, or something that’s a permanent they don’t control.”
    CR 603.2 — “Whenever a game event or game state matches a triggered ability’s trigger event, that ability automatically triggers. The ability doesn’t do anything at this point.”
    CR 603.3 — “Once an ability has triggered, its controller puts it on the stack as an object that’s not a card the next time a player would receive priority. See rule 117, “Timing and Priority.” The ability becomes the topmost object on the stack.”
    CR 603.10a — “Some zone-change triggers look back in time. These are leaves-the-battlefield abilities, abilities that trigger when a player sacrifices a permanent”
    CR 111.7 — “Note that if a token changes zones, applicable triggered abilities will trigger before the token ceases to exist.”
  2. All players pass; the top object - Bastion of Remembrance's drain trigger - resolves. This is drain #1.
    Each of the four opponents loses 1 life: 40 -> 39 each. You gain 1 life: 39 -> 40. Opposing life in the pod: 160 -> 156.
    CR 405.5 — “When all players pass in succession, the top (last-added) spell or ability on the stack resolves.”
    CR 119.3 — “If an effect causes a player to gain life or lose life, that player’s life total is adjusted accordingly.”
  3. All players pass again; Yawgmoth's activated ability resolves. 'Put a -1/-1 counter on up to one target creature and draw a card.' Target: Grizzly Bears.
    You, as the controller of the ability, follow its instructions in the order written: YOU put one -1/-1 counter on Grizzly Bears, which becomes 1/1, and YOU draw a card (library 60 -> 59, hand 0 -> 1). The identity of the player placing the counter is the whole trigger condition of Nest of Scarabs, which reads 'Whenever YOU put one or more -1/-1 counters on a creature, create that many 1/1 black Insect creature tokens' - and because the ability's controller is you, that condition is met and Nest triggers for one counter. It has triggered and has done nothing yet - no token exists.
    CR 608.2c — “The controller of the spell or ability follows its instructions in the order written.”
    CR 122.1a — “A +X/+Y counter on a creature or on a creature card in a zone other than the battlefield, where X and Y are numbers, adds X to that object’s power and Y to that object’s toughness. Similarly, -X/-Y counters subtract from power and toughness.”
    CR 121.1 — “A player draws a card by putting the top card of their library into their hand.”
  4. A player would receive priority, so the game runs state-based actions first, repeats until none is performed, and only then puts waiting triggered abilities on the stack.
    The state-based action check finds nothing to do: Grizzly Bears' toughness is 1, and the state-based action that moves a creature to its owner's graveyard applies only at toughness 0 or less (quoted at step 5, where it is met). Nest of Scarabs' trigger is then put on the stack and resolves, creating Insect token C under your control. ACTIVATION 1 COMPLETE. Bodies you control other than Yawgmoth: Insect B, Human Soldier token, Insect C = 3 (you spent Insect A and got Insect C: net zero, because the counter went onto an opponent's creature). You: 40 life, 59 cards in library, 1 card in hand. Opponents: 39 each.
    CR 117.5 — “Each time a player would get priority, the game first performs all applicable state-based actions as a single event (see rule 704, “State-Based Actions”), then repeats this process until no state-based actions are performed. Then triggered abilities are put on the stack (see rule 603, “Handling Triggered Abilities”).”
  5. ACTIVATION 2, same shape: activate Yawgmoth targeting Grizzly Bears again, pay 1 life (40 -> 39), sacrifice Insect B. Bastion's trigger from Insect B's death resolves first (drain #2: opponents 39 -> 38, you 39 -> 40), then Yawgmoth's ability resolves, putting a second -1/-1 counter on Grizzly Bears (now 0/0) and drawing a card.
    Nest of Scarabs triggers again but has not resolved. Before that trigger can be put on the stack, state-based actions run: Grizzly Bears has toughness 0 and is put into its owner's graveyard - it dies. Bastion of Remembrance does NOT trigger for it: Bastion reads 'Whenever a creature you control dies', and the look-back the trigger performs (step 1) finds Grizzly Bears under opponent A's control at the moment it left the battlefield. Only then is Nest's trigger put on the stack; it resolves and creates Insect token D. This is the same ordering that refuted the near-infinite claim in stack 001: the Insect that REPLACES THE BODY YOU SPENT (Nest is a triggered ability, not a replacement effect - nothing here is replaced in the 614 sense) is created strictly after the death it makes up for, and it never exists while that iteration's target is being chosen. ACTIVATION 2 COMPLETE. Bodies other than Yawgmoth: Human Soldier, Insect C, Insect D = 3 (again net zero - the opponent's creature absorbed the counter). You: 40 life, library 58, 2 cards in hand. Opponents: 38 each.
    CR 117.5 — “Each time a player would get priority, the game first performs all applicable state-based actions as a single event (see rule 704, “State-Based Actions”), then repeats this process until no state-based actions are performed. Then triggered abilities are put on the stack (see rule 603, “Handling Triggered Abilities”).”
    CR 704.5f — “If a creature has toughness 0 or less, it’s put into its owner’s graveyard.”
    CR 700.4 — “The term dies means “is put into a graveyard from the battlefield.””
  6. Census of legal targets for every subsequent activation, now that no opponent controls a creature. Targets for an activated ability are chosen as the ability is activated, from what exists at that moment.
    Three candidates are ruled out and one class remains. (i) Yawgmoth himself is NEVER a legal target of his own ability: he has protection from Humans and he is a Human Cleric, so the ability's source has the stated quality and cannot target him. (ii) Bastion of Remembrance and Nest of Scarabs are Enchantments, not creatures, so they are not 'target creature' at all. (iii) The Insect that Nest of Scarabs will create does not exist when the target is chosen, so it can never be the target of the same activation that creates it. What remains: only the 1/1 tokens you already control - the Human Soldier token (a Human, but protection is a property of Yawgmoth, not a restriction on whom Yawgmoth may target) and any Insect tokens from earlier iterations. Every one of them dies to a single counter, which is what turns the engine body-negative at step 7.
    CR 702.16b — “A permanent or player with protection can’t be targeted by spells with the stated quality and can’t be targeted by abilities from a source with the stated quality.”
    CR 115.1c — “An activated ability is targeted if it identifies something it will affect by using the phrase “target [something],” where the “something” is a phrase that describes an object and/or player. The target(s) are chosen as the ability is activated; see rule 602.2b.”
  7. ACTIVATIONS 3 and 4 - the true engine iteration, where the counter must land on one of your own bodies. Activation 3: pay 1 life (40 -> 39), sacrifice Insect C, target Insect D. Activation 4: pay 1 life (41 -> 40), sacrifice the Human Soldier token, target Insect E.
    Each such activation produces TWO Bastion drains, not one. First the sacrificed creature dies during cost payment (drain), and that trigger resolves before Yawgmoth's ability. Then Yawgmoth's ability resolves and puts a -1/-1 counter on your own 1/1, making it 0/0; Nest of Scarabs triggers. State-based actions then kill that 1/1 - a second creature you control dying, so Bastion triggers again (a token still triggers this before it ceases to exist). Bastion's new trigger and Nest's waiting trigger go on the stack together and you order them freely; both resolve. Net per iteration: minus 2 bodies (one sacrificed, one counter-killed) plus 1 Insect = NET MINUS ONE BODY; 2 drains minus the 1 life paid as a cost (the paying-life rule is cited at step 11, where the totals land) = NET PLUS 1 LIFE for you and MINUS 2 for each opponent. Activation 3 (drains #3, #4): opponents 38 -> 36, you 39 -> 41; bodies left = Human Soldier, Insect E = 2. Activation 4 (drains #5, #6): opponents 36 -> 34, you 40 -> 42; bodies left = Insect F = 1. Library 56, 4 cards in hand.
    CR 704.5f — “If a creature has toughness 0 or less, it’s put into its owner’s graveyard.”
    CR 111.7 — “A token that’s in a zone other than the battlefield ceases to exist. This is a state-based action; see rule 704. (Note that if a token changes zones, applicable triggered abilities will trigger before the token ceases to exist.)”
    CR 603.3b — “First, each player, in APNAP order, puts each triggered ability they control with a trigger condition that isn’t another ability triggering on the stack in any order they choose.”
  8. ACTIVATION 5 - one body left (Insect F), and it is the same body the cost must eat. Targets are announced during the proposal of the ability and costs are paid afterward, so Insect F is still on the battlefield and IS a legal target choice when targets are declared. Two lines exist; take the better one.
    Line A (target Insect F, then sacrifice it to pay the cost): on resolution the ability's only target is no longer in the zone it was in, every instance of 'target' is illegal, and the ability does not resolve - no counter, no Nest trigger, and NO CARD DRAWN. Line B (declare zero targets, which 'up to one target creature' expressly permits - an ability that allows zero targets to be chosen is not targeted at all when none is chosen, so the target-legality check on resolution never bites): the ability resolves, the counter instruction does nothing for want of a target, and you still draw a card; Nest of Scarabs does not trigger because no counter was put on anything, so no Insect replaces Insect F. Line B is strictly better and is the line taken. Either way Insect F's sacrifice gives exactly ONE Bastion drain (#7): pay 1 life (42 -> 41), drain back to 42; opponents 34 -> 33. ACTIVATION 5 COMPLETE. Library 55, 5 cards in hand. Bodies other than Yawgmoth: ZERO.
    CR 601.2c — “If the spell has a variable number of targets, the player announces how many targets they will choose before they announce those targets.”
    CR 601.2h — “The player pays the total cost. First, they pay all costs that don’t involve random elements or moving objects from the library to a public zone, in any order.”
    CR 115.6 — “A spell or ability that requires targets may allow zero targets to be chosen. Such a spell or ability is still said to require targets, but that spell or ability is targeted only if one or more targets have been chosen for it.”
    CR 608.2b — “If the spell or ability specifies targets, it checks whether the targets are still legal. A target that’s no longer in the zone it was in when it was targeted is illegal. Other changes to the game state may cause a target to no longer be legal; for example, its characteristics may have changed or an effect may have changed the text of the spell. If the source of an ability has left the zone it was in, its last known information is used during this process. If all its targets, for every instance of the word “target,” are now illegal, the spell or ability doesn’t resolve.”
  9. Attempt a sixth activation of the SACRIFICE ability. The only creature on the battlefield is Yawgmoth himself, and his cost reads 'Sacrifice another creature'.
    THE SACRIFICE LOOP IS BOUNDED AND IT ENDS HERE, on running out of creatures to sacrifice. You control no creature other than Yawgmoth, so the sacrifice cost is unpayable; partial payment is not allowed, and an activation begun and not legally completable is reversed in full with no abilities triggering. This exhausts the FIRST ability only - Yawgmoth's second ability, '{B}{B}, Discard a card: Proliferate', is still activatable here (three untapped Swamps, five cards in hand); in this line it would simply accomplish nothing, because no permanent and no player carries a counter once the Bears and every counter-bearing token are gone. Note precisely what did NOT end it: (i) not your life - you paid 5 life across five activations and gained 7, ending HIGHER than you started; (ii) not your library - 55 cards remain and the draw-from-an-empty-library loss never comes near; (iii) not the opponent's creature toughness - Grizzly Bears' 2 toughness only EXTENDED the engine, absorbing two counters and letting activations 1 and 2 cost zero net bodies; (iv) not running out of targets in the abstract, since your own Insects are always legal targets - you run out of bodies. The general rule for this board: completed activations = (sacrificeable bodies you start with) + (total toughness of opposing creatures you can put counters on) = 3 + 2 = 5.
    CR 601.2h — “Partial payments are not allowed. Unpayable costs can’t be paid.”
    CR 733.1 — “If a player takes an illegal action or starts to take an action but can’t legally complete it, the entire action is reversed and any payments already made are canceled.”
    CR 121.4 — “A player who attempts to draw a card from a library with no cards in it loses the game the next time a player would receive priority.”
  10. SEPARATE THE BOARD INVARIANT FROM THE LINE. Everything above is the sacrifice engine on its own; the board also offers Yawgmoth's SECOND ability, '{B}{B}, Discard a card: Proliferate', which the three Swamps can pay exactly once (they make {B}{B}{B}, and there is no second {B}{B} after that) and which has a card to discard from activation 1 onward. It does not change the number of completed sacrifice activations, and it does change the drain count.
    BOARD INVARIANT (sacrifice engine alone, no proliferate): 5 completed activations, 7 Bastion triggers, 28 life removed from the pod - the figure reported in final_state. Sister stack 003 also lands on 28, but NOT on this board and NOT by this route, and the difference is the single most useful thing these two stacks teach together. 003 swaps BASTION OF REMEMBRANCE, an enchantment, for ZULAPORT CUTTHROAT, a 1/1 CREATURE, and has no Human Soldier token. That is forced: an enchantment cannot be a body, so this board's third sacrificeable body is the 1/1 white Human Soldier that Bastion's own enters-the-battlefield ability made, while on 003 the drainer IS the third body. Two consequences follow. (i) 003's drainer can die, and the Insect that Nest returns in its place cannot drain, so 003 caps at SEVEN drains even WITH a proliferate; this board's drainer is an enchantment that can never die, which is exactly why one proliferate here reaches EIGHT. (ii) The two 28s are not the same quantity reached the same way: 003's 28 is its ceiling INCLUDING one proliferate, while the 28 here is the NO-proliferate figure and this board still has 32 above it. The figures coincide only because both boards happen to offer three sacrificeable bodies and the same 2-toughness Grizzly Bears, so both run five activations - coincidence of arithmetic, not corroboration, and neither number checks the other. LINE-DEPENDENT VARIANT (one proliferate, taken after activation 1): proliferating the Grizzly Bears - the only permanent then carrying a counter - gives it a second -1/-1 counter. You, as the controller of that ability, are the one putting the counter, so Nest of Scarabs triggers for one Insect exactly as it does off Yawgmoth's first ability, and the Bears dies to a state-based action without any of your creatures being spent on it. That converts the Bears' remaining toughness into a BODY instead of into a free activation: the sacrifice-activation count is still FIVE, but one more iteration now lands its counter on your own creature, giving 8 Bastion drains instead of 7 (you 43, each opponent 32, 32 total drained, library 55, hand 4 after the discard). The two figures must not be blended: 7 / 28 is a property of the board, 8 / 32 is a property of a line that spends {B}{B} and a card.
    CR 701.34a — “To proliferate means to choose any number of permanents and/or players that have a counter, then give each one additional counter of each kind that permanent or player already has.”
    CR 608.2c — “The controller of the spell or ability follows its instructions in the order written.”
  11. Final accounting over all five completed activations of the sacrifice ability (board-invariant line, no proliferate).
    SEVEN creatures you controlled died: Insect A, Insect B, Insect C, the Human Soldier token and Insect F sacrificed as costs (5), plus Insect D and Insect E counter-killed by state-based action on activations 3 and 4 (2). Bastion of Remembrance therefore triggered SEVEN times, one per death. All seven were TOKENS, and the sequence is the mechanism that pays this deck, so state it in order rather than in reverse: each of the seven DID reach the graveyard - the five sacrifices are moved from the battlefield directly to their owner's graveyard by 701.21a (cited at step 1) and the two counter-killed Insects are put there by 704.5f (cited at steps 5 and 7) - and that arrival IS the 'dies' event of 700.4 (cited at step 5), which is the trigger condition every one of the seven Bastion drains keys on. Only afterwards, on the next state-based-action check (117.5, cited at steps 4 and 5), does 704.5d apply: each token is by then in a zone other than the battlefield and ceases to exist. It dies first and ceases to exist second. So the ending graveyard holds none of them and gains no card - graveyard_additions is empty as an END STATE, not because nothing of yours ever reached the graveyard. LIFE: you paid 1 life five times and gained 1 life seven times; the gain-and-lose adjustment for a Bastion drain is established at step 2 and every one of the seven drains has that identical shape. From the 40 you had before activation 1 (39 as the scenario states it, with the first payment already made) you finish at 42 - net +2 over the whole engine, +3 from the stated board. Per completed activation the net change to YOUR life is: 0 on activations 1, 2 and 5 (one drain, one life paid) and +1 on activations 3 and 4, which are the only iterations running in true engine mode (two drains, one life paid). Each of the four opponents loses exactly 7 life, 40 -> 33. Total life removed from the pod: 7 x 4 = 28, from 160 down to 132. LETHALITY, stated as a checked state-based action rather than an impression: the life-total check runs each time a player would receive priority (117.5, step 4) and never fires anywhere in this line - it takes a player at 0 or less life, and the lowest total on the table at any point is 33. Nobody is close to dead; this is a value engine and a sacrifice outlet, not a kill.
    CR 118.3b — “Paying life is done by subtracting the indicated amount of life from a player’s life total.”
    CR 704.5d — “If a token is in a zone other than the battlefield, it ceases to exist.”
    CR 704.5a — “If a player has 0 or less life, that player loses the game.”

A-003 Yawgmoth + Nest of Scarabs + Zulaport Cutthroat ✓ cleared, 3 cycle(s) · 12 steps · 32 citations
Rules version June 19, 2026
The question put to the record
Resolve the stack, then run the engine as far as the rules allow, playing to kill the table. (a) Zulaport Cutthroat is a CREATURE and therefore a legal target for Yawgmoth's ability and a legal sacrifice for its cost. State whether the pilot is ever FORCED to consume it, or whether it can be preserved throughout, and give the exact number of completed activations from this board. (b) Zulaport drains EACH opponent. How much total life do all four opponents lose, and is that lethal?
  1. Fix the reading first, then do the bookkeeping the stack picture omits. THE READING: the scenario's board annotates 'Insect token A' as already sacrificed to pay the cost of the ability now on the stack, matching the house convention used in stack 001 — the board list names the cost-payment body, it is NOT still on the battlefield, and the pilot's live insect count entering this resolution is ONE (Insect token B). Every number below is derived under that reading; the n=4 reading in which both A and B are alive is not the scenario as written. BOOKKEEPING: paying the cost sacrificed Insect token A, moving it from the battlefield to the graveyard, which is a 'dies' event. Zulaport Cutthroat's ability is a look-back-in-time trigger and sees that death even though Insect A is a TOKEN and a later state-based action removes it from the graveyard. That trigger is put on the stack ABOVE the activated ability and therefore resolves first.
    Stack (top-down): Zulaport Cutthroat's death trigger; Yawgmoth's -1/-1 ability targeting Grizzly Bears. You are at 39 (1 life paid); opponents at 40 each. Insect A reached your graveyard (making it a legal 'dies' event) and then ceased to exist as a token — which is why it will never appear in the final graveyard list even though it drained the table.
    CR 701.21a — “To sacrifice a permanent, its controller moves it from the battlefield directly to its owner’s graveyard.”
    CR 700.4 — “The term dies means “is put into a graveyard from the battlefield.””
    CR 704.5d — “If a token is in a zone other than the battlefield, it ceases to exist.”
    CR 603.10a — “Some zone-change triggers look back in time. These are leaves-the-battlefield abilities, abilities that trigger when a player sacrifices a permanent”
    CR 603.3 — “Once an ability has triggered, its controller puts it on the stack as an object that’s not a card the next time a player would receive priority.”
  2. Zulaport Cutthroat's trigger resolves: 'each opponent loses 1 life and you gain 1 life.' Four opponents, so four separate life losses.
    Opponent A/B/C/D: 40 -> 39 each (4 total life lost). You: 39 -> 40. Death count of creatures you control: 1.
    CR 608.2c — “The controller of the spell or ability follows its instructions in the order written.”
    CR 119.3 — “If an effect causes a player to gain life or lose life, that player’s life total is adjusted accordingly.”
  3. Yawgmoth's activated ability resolves. Before it does anything, its target is rechecked for legality: Grizzly Bears is still on the battlefield and nothing has changed its characteristics, so it is still a legal target and the ability resolves normally. Put one -1/-1 counter on it and draw a card.
    Grizzly Bears is 1/1 with one -1/-1 counter — toughness above 0, so it survives. Library 60 -> 59, hand 1. Putting that counter triggers Nest of Scarabs.
    CR 608.2b — “If the spell or ability specifies targets, it checks whether the targets are still legal.”
    CR 122.1a — “Similarly, -X/-Y counters subtract from power and toughness.”
  4. State-based actions are checked (nothing dies: Bears is 1/1), then Nest of Scarabs' trigger is put on the stack. All players pass priority in succession — the scenario's stated assumption — so it resolves, creating one 1/1 black Insect token (Insect C). This same all-pass condition is what lets every object that resolves across this main phase actually resolve, and it is not restated below.
    Your board: Yawgmoth 2/4, Nest of Scarabs, Zulaport Cutthroat 1/1, Insect B, Insect C. Critically, Insect C could NOT have been the target of the activation that produced it — targets for that ability were announced when it was put on the stack, when Insect C did not exist. This is the same lock that refuted stack 001.
    CR 117.5 — “Each time a player would get priority, the game first performs all applicable state-based actions as a single event (see rule 704, “State-Based Actions”), then repeats this process until no state-based actions are performed. Then triggered abilities are put on the stack (see rule 603, “Handling Triggered Abilities”).”
    CR 117.4 — “If all players pass in succession (that is, if all players pass without taking any actions in between passing), the spell or ability on top of the stack resolves”
    CR 601.2c — “The player announces their choice of an appropriate object or player for each target the spell requires.”
    CR 602.2b — “The remainder of the process for activating an ability is identical to the process for casting a spell listed in rules 601.2b–i. Those rules apply to activating an ability just as they apply to casting a spell.”
  5. Answer (a), the load-bearing question: is the pilot ever FORCED to consume Zulaport Cutthroat? No — on three independent grounds. (i) Yawgmoth's ability is an ACTIVATED ability: the pilot may activate it, and is never compelled to, unlike stack 001's mandatory triggered ability. (ii) The cost is 'Sacrifice another creature': the pilot pays the cost and any creature they control other than Yawgmoth is a full payment, so an Insect token satisfies it whenever one exists. (iii) The effect is 'up to one target creature', so choosing ZERO targets is legal — the counter can never be forced onto Zulaport.
    Zulaport Cutthroat is preserved through every iteration in which any Insect token exists. It is consumed only at the very end, and only because the pilot ELECTS to cash it in for one final drain once it is the last sacrificeable body. Nothing in the engine can take it involuntarily.
    CR 113.3b — “A player may activate such an ability whenever they have priority.”
    CR 601.2h — “The player pays the total cost.”
    CR 115.6 — “A spell or ability that requires targets may allow zero targets to be chosen.”
  6. Establish the loop's hard ceiling before running it. The engine is body-neutral only while some creature can absorb a -1/-1 counter and live: each activation spends one body (sacrifice) and Nest returns at most one body (one counter, one token). Every creature you control except Yawgmoth is a 1/1, so a counter on your own board kills the body it lands on. Yawgmoth is 2/4 and could absorb three counters — but he has protection from Humans and the source of his own activated ability is Yawgmoth, a Human Cleric, so he can never be targeted by it.
    The only sink that can take a counter and survive is Grizzly Bears (2/2), and it can absorb exactly two -1/-1 counters before dying. After Bears is gone every activation is net -1 body. The loop is therefore strictly FINITE, not 'near-infinite'.
    CR 702.16b — “A permanent or player with protection can’t be targeted by spells with the stated quality and can’t be targeted by abilities from a source with the stated quality.”
    CR 113.7 — “The source of an activated ability on the stack is the object whose ability was activated.”
    CR 704.5f — “If a creature has toughness 0 or less, it’s put into its owner’s graveyard.”
  7. Convert the three untapped Swamps — the resource the scenario deliberately supplies, since the -1/-1 ability costs no mana. Activate Yawgmoth's second ability: {B}{B}, Discard a card (the card drawn in step 3): Proliferate. Choose Grizzly Bears, the only permanent in the game with a counter on it, and give it a second -1/-1 counter.
    Bears becomes 0/0. You put a -1/-1 counter on a creature, so Nest of Scarabs triggers. By the step 4 sequencing, SBAs run first and Bears is put into its owner's graveyard (704.5f, established in step 6) — it is an OPPONENT'S creature and Zulaport reads 'another creature YOU control', so Zulaport does not trigger, and Bears is a card, not a token, so it stays in the graveyard — then the Nest trigger resolves for Insect token D. Net: one extra body for {B}{B} and a discard, with no sacrifice spent. Mana remaining {B} (a second proliferate is unaffordable). Your bodies: Insect B, C, D + Zulaport = 4.
    CR 701.34a — “To proliferate means to choose any number of permanents and/or players that have a counter, then give each one additional counter of each kind that permanent or player already has.”
  8. Activation #2 of the -1/-1 ability, and the step that puts the TOKEN-DEATH mechanic on the record. Pay 1 life, sacrifice Insect B (a token — it moves to the graveyard, which is the 'dies' event, and only ceases to exist afterwards), target Insect C. On resolution, put a -1/-1 counter on Insect C and draw. Insect C is now 0/0, and SBAs run as a single event: 704.5f puts Insect C into its owner's graveyard, and only on the NEXT SBA check — the repeat that 117.5 requires — is 704.5d applicable, because the token is only then in a zone other than the battlefield. That ordering is why each token death is a real 'dies' event Zulaport can see; a token that never reached the graveyard would produce one drain in this whole line, not seven. Two abilities have now triggered since the last priority — Zulaport's second death trigger and Nest of Scarabs' token trigger — and the pilot controls both, so the pilot chooses the order they go on the stack; both orders reach the same totals here, because Nest's replacement token can never become the target of an activation whose targets were already locked (step 4). This same simultaneous-trigger choice recurs in step 9.
    Two drains this activation. Opponents 39 -> 37 each; you 40 -> 41 (paid 1, gained 2). Library 58, hand 1. Insect B and Insect C both hit the graveyard and both ceased to exist. Nest's trigger creates Insect E. Bodies: Insect D, Insect E + Zulaport. Death count 3.
    CR 704.5f — “If a creature has toughness 0 or less, it’s put into its owner’s graveyard.”
    CR 704.5d — “If a token is in a zone other than the battlefield, it ceases to exist.”
    CR 117.5 — “Each time a player would get priority, the game first performs all applicable state-based actions as a single event (see rule 704, “State-Based Actions”), then repeats this process until no state-based actions are performed. Then triggered abilities are put on the stack (see rule 603, “Handling Triggered Abilities”).”
    CR 603.3b — “First, each player, in APNAP order, puts each triggered ability they control with a trigger condition that isn’t another ability triggering on the stack in any order they choose.”
    CR 119.3 — “If an effect causes a player to gain life or lose life, that player’s life total is adjusted accordingly.”
  9. Activation #3, identical shape to step 8 in every respect — same sacrifice-death, same 704.5f-then-704.5d token disposal, same pilot-ordered simultaneous triggers, same life adjustments. Pay 1 life, sacrifice Insect D, target Insect E; the counter takes Insect E to 0/0 and it is put into the graveyard; Nest creates Insect F.
    Two more drains. Opponents 37 -> 35 each; you 41 -> 42. Library 57, hand 2. Bodies: Insect F + Zulaport. Death count 5.
    CR 704.5f — “If a creature has toughness 0 or less, it’s put into its owner’s graveyard.”
  10. Activation #4. Pay 1 life, sacrifice Insect F. Now the target choice matters: the only creatures left would be Zulaport Cutthroat and Yawgmoth. Yawgmoth cannot be targeted, and the pilot declines to kill Zulaport, so choose ZERO targets — legal because the ability reads 'up to one target creature'. With no target chosen there is nothing for the on-resolution legality check of step 3 to examine, so the ability resolves and simply draws.
    One drain (Insect F, a token death by sacrifice as in step 1). No counter is placed, so Nest of Scarabs does not trigger and no token is made. Opponents 35 -> 34 each; you 42 -> 42 (paid 1, gained 1). Library 56, hand 3. Bodies: Zulaport Cutthroat only. Death count 6. (Targeting Zulaport here instead would trade the same total — it dies for a drain and Nest replaces it with a body that can no longer drain — so 7 is the ceiling either way.)
    CR 115.6 — “A spell or ability that requires targets may allow zero targets to be chosen.”
  11. Activation #5, the final one, and the literal subject of the scenario's title: does the drain survive its own engine? Pay 1 life, sacrifice Zulaport Cutthroat itself. Its ability reads 'Whenever this creature or another creature you control dies' — a leaves-the-battlefield ability, and such abilities look back in time to the game state immediately before the event, so Zulaport sees the very sacrifice that removed it and its trigger goes on the stack. Zero targets again (only Yawgmoth remains and he is untargetable). Draw. Then the loop ENDS: Yawgmoth is the only creature you control and the cost demands ANOTHER creature, so activation #6 has no legal payment.
    Seventh and last drain. Opponents 34 -> 33 each; you 42. Library 55, hand 4. Zulaport is a card, not a token, so it remains in the graveyard. Board: Yawgmoth, Nest of Scarabs, three Swamps. Total completed activations: FIVE of the -1/-1 ability (including the one that started on the stack) plus ONE proliferate activation. What ends the loop is body exhaustion under 'Sacrifice ANOTHER creature' — not life, not the library.
    CR 603.6c — “Leaves-the-battlefield abilities trigger when a permanent moves from the battlefield to another zone, or when a phased-in permanent leaves the game because its owner leaves the game.”
    CR 603.10a — “Some zone-change triggers look back in time. These are leaves-the-battlefield abilities, abilities that trigger when a player sacrifices a permanent”
    CR 118.3 — “A player can’t pay a cost without having the necessary resources to pay it fully.”
    CR 601.2h — “Unpayable costs can’t be paid.”
  12. Final state-based-action check and the arithmetic for (b). Seven creatures you controlled died while Zulaport Cutthroat was on the battlefield or, on the last one, was looking back at its own departure (Insect A, B, C, D, E, F and Zulaport itself). Each death drained EACH of four opponents for 1. Grizzly Bears is excluded: it belongs to opponent A and Zulaport reads 'another creature YOU control'.
    Each opponent loses 7 life: 40 -> 33. Total life removed from the table: 7 x 4 = 28. Opposing life 160 -> 132. No opponent is at 0 or less life, so no player loses the game; you never came near decking (60 -> 55). NOT lethal, and not close — the line removes 17.5% of the table's life. The 'near-infinite' family tag is wrong here for the second time on this deck.
    CR 704.5a — “If a player has 0 or less life, that player loses the game.”
